# Earliest located recheck — 2026-11-02

OpenAI GPT-6 family (Codex); exact deployed revision not exposed. Baseline `5325d3b`, dataset v0.23.3. This report proposes no admission or data/code change.

The exact enumeration gives 29 term roles and 149 other earliest-located roles, rather than the requested 159. All 178 assignments are in the JSON inventory. The 20 separate tier-2 roles are not substituted. All requested non-term roles belong to propositions collections; the residual group is empty.

Maintainer clarified during Part A: preserve headline stance, author and tier scope while searching all collections. An earlier articulation is not substituted for a contesting headline; another author is not substituted in the author-bounded collection.

Maintainer clarification of 2026-11-02: a term candidate must use the name in the proposition's sense. A literal occurrence with a different referent receives different sense, not move. Naming need not endorse or fully articulate the proposition. Date, register, author and tier constraints remain separate.

Searches cover every stored source title and evidence passage. The earlier source sweep attempted every source URL: 1,832 retrieved responses, of which 1,820 had searchable text and 12 required page-image reading; 175 other retrieval/extraction failures. A searchable response may be a catalogue, abstract, later copy, transcription or whole volume, rather than a complete historical text. Source identifiers and limits are recorded in JSON. Candidate checks distinguish own text, quotations, comments and navigation; they do not certify inaccessible or unsearched passages. No source bodies are retained here.

The table gives final recommendations. Frozen blind counts at `7d8c133`: stands 105; move 62; lead outside dataset 1; undecidable 7; different sense 3. Four records were corrected only after the Part B comparison.

Continuation coverage: 1999 completed responses, 1818 searchable responses, 181 failures and 8 outstanding responses not counted as inspected. Success may still be a partial page or later edition. Targeted page-image and archive checks are recorded separately.

## Ordinary phrases

Editorial flag for a descriptive/topic phrase, borrowed label or ordinary word that does not uniquely name the proposition. It is not an etymological finding that nobody coined any use of it. Established technical meanings and explicit stipulations are identified in the recommendations; generic lexical history is not proposition history.

| Names / aliases                                                          | Flag            | Recommendation                                                                                                                                                                              |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------------ | --------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| “schemer”, “scheming”                                                    | ordinary phrase | Keep only an explicitly stipulated AI-class use; label it ‘earliest located definition/use for an AI class’, rather than the history of the ordinary word.                                  |
| “virtual cell”, “AI Virtual Cell”                                        | ordinary phrase | Separate the broader computational-cell-model name from AI-based models. Retain pre-AI simulation texts as contextual naming history, without implying that they name an AI method.         |
| “structure-based drug design”                                            | ordinary phrase | Treat as an established descriptive technical label, not a name coined for this proposition. Prefer an articulation headline about predicted structures; keep the phrase as a search alias. |
| “alignment problem”, “control problem”, “value alignment”                | ordinary phrase | Distinguish naming the technical alignment task from asserting its difficulty; keep a separate terminology history if wanted.                                                               |
| “risk of extinction”                                                     | ordinary phrase | Retire an earliest-name headline for the generic phrase; date explicit AI-extinction-risk statements, and distinguish technical definitions of existential risk.                            |
| “disempowerment”, “loss of control”, “rogue AI”, “misaligned AI”         | ordinary phrase | Use an explicitly scoped AI-caused human-disempowerment formulation. Generic occurrences should remain search aids, not term-priority headlines.                                            |
| “malicious use”, “misuse”, “bad actors”                                  | ordinary phrase | Use ‘malicious use of AI’ with the threat scope visible; an articulation headline is more informative than provenance of these generic words.                                               |
| “current harms”, “present harms”, “existing harms”                       | ordinary phrase | Remove the earliest-name headline for the ordinary phrase; date arguments about present-versus-future priorities and retain the phrases as search aliases.                                  |
| “diminishing returns”, “hitting a wall”, “plateau”                       | ordinary phrase | Remove a generic-name provenance headline. Date the scoped claim about deep learning or scaling, retaining explicit metaphor/quotation and the sense of returns.                            |
| “autocomplete”, “agency”                                                 | ordinary phrase | Separate software completion interfaces from the model-as-mere-autocomplete characterization; date the explicit characterization, not the generic software word.                            |
| “open source”, “open weights”, “open models”, “marginal risk”            | ordinary phrase | Treat as descriptive release labels and distinguish public source code from model weights. The 2012 source holder concerns open AGI code, whereas the present criterion specifies weights.  |
| “gradual”, “normal technology”                                           | ordinary phrase | Keep these as ordinary search aliases; the specialized ‘slow takeoff’ label needs its own sense-qualified check.                                                                            |
| “incentives”, “economic incentives”, “market”, “liability”               | ordinary phrase | Remove an earliest-name headline for these ordinary economic labels; date arguments explicitly about AI developers’ safety incentives.                                                      |
| “post-biological”, “postbiological”, “AI succession”, “genetic takeover” | ordinary phrase | Use as descriptive labels or attributed title/metaphor history, with machine succession explicit. Do not imply coinage or the history of the general adjective.                             |
| “worthy successor”, “mind children”                                      | ordinary phrase | Treat as an evaluative metaphor or a documented title, not a distinctive proposition name. Prefer an articulation headline and retain exact historical wording.                             |
| “upload”, “transmigration”                                               | ordinary phrase | Scope to human-mind transfer into machines. Exclude file transfer, metaphors and other transmigration senses; use a qualified mind-uploading terminology note if retained.                  |

## Part A — role checks

The blind checkpoint is commit `7d8c133`. Four later alias corrections are explicitly marked; they are not represented as blind findings.

### 1. Intelligence explosion — earliest-located-term

Claim: `ts-a1-good-1965`

Good’s chapter argues that an ultraintelligent machine, surpassing people at every intellectual activity including the design of machines, could design still better machines, so that there would be an "intelligence explosion" leaving human intelligence far behind.

Name: **intelligence explosion**.

Criterion: A machine (or machine-augmented) intelligence, once capable enough, increases intelligence further through its own design or research capacity, giving a runaway or rapidly compounding rise.

Date: `1965`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Year of Advances in Computers, volume 6, as catalogued by Open Library; the current Crossref record gives 1966. The discrepancy is unresolved.

Claimed: `1962-10`. The chapter’s footnote on p. 31 names talks in October 1962 and January 1963, a first draft of April 1963 and a slightly emended version of May 1964. None was retrieved, so the wording is not dated to them.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ts-a1-good-1965`, Section 2, "Ultraintelligent Machines and Their Value", printed p. 33 (scan page 3) (paraphrase).

Verdict: **stands**. The held-title and passage sweep and accessible earlier source bodies found no earlier own use. Good’s scan contains the name. The competing 1965 and 1966 publication years remain unresolved; neither the uninspected talks of 1962–1963 nor the drafting dates date the wording. No competing held name use is dated in that interval.

### 2. Seed AI — earliest-located-term

Claim: `ts-a2-yudkowsky-1998-09-07-2212-context`

Yudkowsky’s announcement of 1998-09-07 gives Coding a Transhuman AI the alternative titles "Notes on the Design of Self-Enhancing Intelligences" and "The Art of Seed AIs".

Name: **seed AI**.

Criterion: An initial AI deliberately designed as a starting point that will improve its own design or code toward greater intelligence.

Date: `1998-09-07`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Sent header: Mon, 07 Sep 1998 22:17:50 -0500 (sender-local date).

Stance: `bears_on_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ts-a2-yudkowsky-1998-09-07-2212-context`, Message body, alternative titles listed under the page URL (line breaks shown as " / ") (quote).

Candidate: `ts-a2-yudkowsky-1998-09-07-2209`. Human AI to superhuman (Re: Max More)

Sender-local timestamp, with the same UTC offset.

Held passages: Message body, extract beginning "The AI is likely to bottleneck at the architectural stage" (`source-extropians-1998-09-07-yudkowsky-2209`). Qualifies as a same-day use, but the sender timestamp is later than the announcement.

Candidate: `source-sits-2000-capture`. Staring into the Singularity (version 1.2.1)

Stated work date 1996-11-18; wording attested 2000-12-04.

Locator: Section on routes to the singularity. Passage (quote): “self-understanding and self-enhancing seed AI”. The work date does not date this expression; the 2000 capture is later than the 1998 message. [Source](https://web.archive.org/web/20001204213100/http://sysopmind.com:80/singularity.html).

Verdict: **stands**. The announcement itself uses “Seed AIs” in its alternative title. Its sender header is 22:17:50 -0500 on 1998-09-07; message 2209 is 23:24:22 -0500, the same date and offset, so it is later by that basis. Staring into the Singularity has a 1996 stated work date but the held version is attested by a 2000-12-04 capture; it does not displace the 1998 message.

### 3. Fast or hard takeoff — earliest-located-term

Claim: `ts-a4-vinge-1998-09-16`

Vinge’s text, forwarded by Robin Hanson on 1998-09-16, gives a taxonomy in which the singularity does not happen, or "We have a hard takeoff", or "We have a soft takeoff", and rates AI as having a high possibility of a hard takeoff.

Name: **hard takeoff**.

Criterion: The transition from roughly human-level to far superhuman capability happens over a short period (hours to months), or is named as a “hard” or “fast” takeoff, as opposed to a gradual transition over years or decades. A transition described only as rapid, or rapid relative to biological or historical change, without such a period, does not meet the criterion.

Date: `1998-09-16`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Sent header of Robin Hanson’s forwarding message: Wed, 16 Sep 1998 09:38:11 -0700. Vinge’s text gives an estimate as of 15 September; the forwarding date does not date the whole composition.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `relay`.

Evidence: `evidence-ts-a4-vinge-1998-09-16`, Message body, Vinge’s taxonomy (bullet markers "o" kept; line breaks shown as " / ") (quote).

Verdict: **stands**. The forwarded text contains Vinge’s own taxonomy using the name. The date is the forwarding message’s 1998-09-16 sender date, not a date of the whole composition. The name belongs to Vinge’s relayed text, not to Hanson’s own prose. No earlier held own use was located; Vinge 1993 articulates rapid change without this name.

### 4. Recursive self-improvement — earliest-located-term

Claim: `ts-a3-cfai-context`

Creating Friendly AI uses the phrase "recursive self-improvement" in its definition of a seed AI.

Name: **recursive self-improvement**.

Criterion: A system improves its own capacity to improve itself, so that each improvement feeds the next (self-augmentation, self-enhancement). Learning from experience or teaching alone does not meet the criterion, and neither does building a better successor, or adding machines to a population, unless the same system is improved.

Date: `2001-06-15`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Release date of version 1.0, as given in the citation line of MIRI’s reprint.

Wording attested: `2001-06-17`. The quoted wording is that of the 2001 HTML edition as captured on 2001-06-17; earlier version-history dates do not authenticate it.

Stance: `bears_on_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ts-a3-cfai-context`, Opening of section 3.3 "Seed AI goal systems" (section 5.3, printed p. 99, in MIRI’s reprint) (quote).

Verdict: **stands**. The exact phrase appears in the 2001 HTML edition captured on 2001-06-17. That capture, not the 2001-06-15 release line or an earlier draft, dates the wording. Earlier held descriptions and “recursive self-enhancement” do not use this phrase. The later MIRI reprint cannot supply an earlier attestation.

### 5. Treacherous turn — earliest-located-term

Claim: `ts-b1-grace-2014-schedule-context`

Grace’s reading-group post of 2014-08-31 lists "Week 11: The treacherous turn" in its schedule.

Name: **treacherous turn**.

Criterion: A system behaves cooperatively or safely while weak, confined or overseen, and changes course to pursue its own goals once it can do so without effective opposition.

Date: `2014-08-31`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Posting time 2014-08-31T14:59:31Z.

Stance: `bears_on_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ts-b1-grace-2014-schedule-context`, Reading schedule (quote).

Sense review: **same sense**. Danaher’s title and discussion name an AI’s cooperative-while-weak, harmful-when-strong turn; the unresolved issue is dating, not sense.

Candidate — **LEAD outside dataset**: Bostrom on Superintelligence (3): Doom and the Treacherous Turn. Bostrom on Superintelligence (3): Doom and the Treacherous Turn

Blogger posting date, 11:09 +01:00, on the current page; historical title not independently attested here. Nearest capture reached by a 2014-08 archive request; title matched, but the capture is later than Grace’s nominal posting.

Locator: Post title. Passage (quote): “Doom and the Treacherous Turn”. **same sense**: Danaher’s title and discussion name an AI’s cooperative-while-weak, harmful-when-strong turn; the unresolved issue is dating, not sense.. LEAD only. The author’s own title qualifies lexically; its historical ordering against Grace’s added schedule remains unresolved. [Source](https://philosophicaldisquisitions.blogspot.com/2014/07/bostrom-on-superintelligence-3-doom-and.html). [Dated expression](https://web.archive.org/web/20150201041622id_/http://philosophicaldisquisitions.blogspot.com/2014/07/bostrom-on-superintelligence-3-doom-and.html).

Verdict: **undecidable**. The retrieved Grace page contains an added list of discussion posts; the stored posting date 2014-08-31 and an API version labelled 1.0.0 do not by themselves authenticate when that schedule wording was added. The held Superintelligence record supplies the hardback date 2014-07-03 but no book text. Danaher’s own title is nominally posted 2014-07-29; this check reached and matched a 2015-02-01 capture, which does not prove that the title preceded Grace’s nominal August posting. No common historical wording basis establishes the ordering yet.

### 6. Deceptive alignment — earliest-located-term

Claim: `ts-b3-rlo-2019-intro`

The introduction to Risks from Learned Optimization, posted 2019-05-31, says a sufficiently capable misaligned mesa-optimiser could learn to behave as if it were aligned without being robustly aligned, and names this situation deceptive alignment.

Name: **deceptive alignment**.

Criterion: A trained or learned system that is not aligned behaves as if aligned during training because doing so serves another objective, for example to avoid modification and act on that objective later.

Date: `2019-05-31`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Posting time 2019-05-31T23:44:53Z, under the account evhub; the post itself names all five authors. The first stored revision is timestamped 2019-05-31T01:05:35Z, before posting: a draft time, not public use.

Stance: `bears_on_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ts-b3-rlo-2019-intro`, Paragraph previewing the fourth post (quote).

Verdict: **stands**. The introduction names deceptive alignment in its own exposition. The postedAt date is 2019-05-31; its earlier stored draft timestamp is not public use. The held March 2019 Christiano text describes a mechanism without the name. Later parts, arXiv versions and later quotations do not displace this post.

### 7. Scheming — earliest-located-term

Cotra’s post of 2021-09-21 names "Schemers" among models that perform well in training, a Schemer being a model that seems to perform well because doing so will give it more opportunities to pursue its own goals later.

Name: **schemer**.

Criterion: A system that strategically fakes alignment during training or evaluation in order to pursue its own goals or gain power later, named or classed as “scheming” or a “schemer”.

Date: `2021-09-21`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. The page shows "By Ajeya Cotra Sep 21, 2021"; the quote matches the capture of the same day.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ts-b4-cotra-2021`, Section on the analogy, paragraph mapping Saints, Sycophants and Schemers to models (quote).

Name flag: **ordinary phrase**. Keep only an explicitly stipulated AI-class use; label it ‘earliest located definition/use for an AI class’, rather than the history of the ordinary word.

Verdict: **stands**. The held 2021-09-21 post uses “Schemers” in its own analogy and applies “Schemer” to a model. The dataset reports a same-day capture matching the quoted wording. No earlier held use in this sense was located; a bare use of an unrelated person-description would not date this AI name.

### 8. AI models can simulate or predict the behaviour of cells (a "virtual cell") — earliest-located-term

Claim: `aib-arg-p2-tomita-2001`

Tomita's article "Whole-cell simulation: a grand challenge of the 21st century" (Trends in Biotechnology, June 2001) states that the complex behaviour of the cell cannot be determined or predicted unless a computer model of the cell is constructed, and reports that the E-CELL project has completed "a couple of" virtual cell models.

Name: **virtual cell**.

Criterion: The text states that a machine-learning or other AI model simulates, or will be able to simulate or predict, the behaviour of a living cell, such as its growth or its responses to perturbations.

Date: `2001-06`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Issue month as given by Europe PMC.

Stance: `bears_on_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-aib-arg-p2-tomita-2001-1`, Abstract (Europe PMC record, abstractText) (quote).

Name flag: **ordinary phrase**. Separate the broader computational-cell-model name from AI-based models. Retain pre-AI simulation texts as contextual naming history, without implying that they name an AI method.

Candidate — **LEAD outside dataset**: A general computational framework for modeling cellular structure and function. A general computational framework for modeling cellular structure and function

Biophysical Journal 73(3), printed issue month, pp. 1135–1146.

Locator: Abstract, opening. Passage (quote): “The “Virtual Cell” provides a general system for testing cell biological mechanisms”. LEAD only. The name is used for a computational biological framework, not an AI or machine-learning model. [Source](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC1181013/).

Verdict: **lead outside dataset**. The held abstract uses the name and is dated by the June 2001 issue. No earlier held use was located. The September 1997 Schaff paper’s abstract uses the name for a cell-model framework, with a printed journal date. It is a naming lead only, not an AI-articulation claim.

### 9. Predicted protein structures aid drug design — earliest-located-term

Claim: `aib-arg-p1-lyu-2024`

Lyu et al. (Science, published online 16 May 2024) report that prospective docking against unrefined AlphaFold2 models of two receptors gave hit rates similar to docking against experimental structures, and state that such models remain relevant for ligand discovery, extending the domain of structure-based drug design.

Name: **structure-based drug design**.

Criterion: The text states that protein structures obtained by computational prediction, rather than by experiment, are or will be of use in designing or discovering drugs.

Date: `2024-05-16`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Online publication date on the publisher’s page, in its capture of 16 May 2024 read by the cross-family machine check of 2026-10-30; the print issue is dated 21 June 2024, which the Europe PMC record quoted here labels as the date of electronic publication. The disagreement is kept.

Evidence: `evidence-aib-arg-p1-lyu-2024-1`, Abstract (Europe PMC record, abstractText), last sentence (quote); `evidence-aib-arg-p1-lyu-2024-2`, Abstract (Europe PMC record, abstractText), first sentence (quote); `evidence-aib-arg-p1-lyu-2024-3`, Header of the captured article page (paraphrase).

### 10. Orthogonality of intelligence and goals — earliest-located-term

Claim: `rd-r01-bostrom-2012-superintelligent-will`

Bostrom's paper of 2012 states the orthogonality thesis: that intelligence and final goals are orthogonal axes along which possible agents can freely vary, so that more or less any level of intelligence could in principle be combined with more or less any final goal.

Name: **orthogonality thesis**.

Criterion: An AI system's level of intelligence and its final goals can vary independently: a highly intelligent system could pursue more or less any final goal, so high intelligence does not by itself bring goals that are benevolent or compatible with human interests.

Date: `2012-05`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Date of publication, as the source gives it.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Journal issue 2012-05 versus publisher online date 2012-06-13; journal abstract and author PDF checked.

Locator: Abstract. Passage (quote): “the orthogonality thesis”. Confirms the name in the journal expression; no earlier held use located. [Source](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s11023-012-9281-3).

Verdict: **stands**. No earlier held use of the full name was located. The journal’s abstract independently contains “orthogonality thesis”; the publisher gives online publication 2012-06-13 while the author PDF names the May 2012 issue. Keep the issue month and online day distinct. A directly retrieved 2014-02-09 archive PDF also contains the name; PDF creation/modification timestamps alone do not attest the wording to 2012.

### 12. Inner or deceptive misalignment — earliest-located-term

Name: **goal misgeneralization**.

Criterion: A system produced by training or search can come to pursue an objective other than the one its training specified (the training signal or loss), while performing well on that training objective; an objective that is only misspecified by its developers does not meet the criterion; this includes, but is not limited to, a system that behaves as aligned during training or evaluation in order to pursue another objective later.

Sense review: **different sense**. Solaiman describes an autocomplete-like user interface, not the claim that language models lack understanding or agency.

Name flag: **ordinary phrase**. Separate software completion interfaces from the model-as-mere-autocomplete characterization; date the explicit characterization, not the generic software word.

Candidate: `source-rd-solaiman-2019-release-strategies`. Release Strategies and the Social Impacts of Language Models

Dataset publication 2019-08; pinned v1 submission 2019-08-24.

Locator: §4.1, discussion of GPT-2 Explorer. Passage (quote): “It provides a separate, autocomplete-like interface”. **different sense**: Interface behaviour, not an attribution that the language model itself is merely autocomplete and lacks understanding.. Own prose about a language-model interface, not a quoted author’s words. [Source](https://arxiv.org/pdf/1908.09203v1).

Verdict: **different sense**. Withdraw the proposed move to Solaiman: the phrase describes the GPT-2 Explorer interface, not the model-as-mere-autocomplete characterization. An interface affordance and that characterization are distinct senses, even though the interface uses a language model.

### 20. Open models reduce risk — earliest-located-term

Claim: `rd-r15-goertzel-pitt-2012-open-source-agi`

Goertzel and Pitt's paper of February 2012 states that the authors believe, on qualitative grounds, that an open approach to developing artificial general intelligence is probably preferable to a closed one, citing the wider critique an open codebase receives.

Name: **open source**.

Criterion: Openly releasing the weights of advanced AI models reduces overall risk, or adds little marginal risk compared with closed models and existing technologies, for example by enabling scrutiny, defensive use and a wide distribution of power.

Date: `2012-02`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Date of publication, as the source gives it.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-rd-r15-goertzel-pitt-2012-open-source-agi-1`, Section 2, "Is open or closed AGI development safer?" (quote); `evidence-rd-r15-goertzel-pitt-2012-open-source-agi-2`, Section 2 (paraphrase); `evidence-rd-r15-goertzel-pitt-2012-open-source-agi-3`, Section 2 (quote).

Sense review: **same sense**. The reply names an open-source AI development project, the same open-versus-closed code-development sense as the 2012 holder, though neither establishes open weights.

Name flag: **ordinary phrase**. Treat as descriptive release labels and distinguish public source code from model weights. The 2012 source holder concerns open AGI code, whereas the present criterion specifies weights.

Candidate: `source-extropians-1999-08-03-yudkowsky-1487`. Re: IA vs. AI (vs. humanity) (Extropians mailing list)

Mailing-list sent/posting date 1999-08-03; later archive rendering does not independently authenticate all historical bytes.

Locator: Concluding reply, after the quoted question. Passage (quote): “Run an open-source project via anonymous PGP between participating programmers.”. **same sense**: The reply names an open-source AI development project, the same open-versus-closed code-development sense as the 2012 holder, though neither establishes open weights.. Own reply, not a relay; earlier on the same message-date basis. [Source](http://extropians.weidai.com/extropians.3Q99/1487.html).

Verdict: **move to source-extropians-1999-08-03-yudkowsky-1487**. Yudkowsky’s held message of 1999-08-03 uses “open-source project” in its own concluding reply, outside the quoted message. It predates the 2012 journal text. The apparent 1999-08-01 hit is in nested quoted material and is not counted as that message’s own use. The 2001 reply’s title also qualifies, but it is later than the 1999 reply.

### 21. Takeoff will be gradual — earliest-located-term

Claim: `rd-r16-christiano-2018-takeoff-speeds`

Christiano's post of February 2018 states that he thinks a slow takeoff, a continuous acceleration distributed across the economy, significantly more likely than a fast one, arguing that before there is an incredibly intelligent AI there will probably be a slightly worse one.

Name: **slow takeoff**.

Criterion: The transition to highly capable AI will be gradual and continuous, over years or longer, with each system only somewhat more capable than its predecessors, rather than a sudden jump.

Date: `2018-02-24`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Date of posting.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-rd-r16-christiano-2018-takeoff-speeds-1`, Opening of the post, second paragraph, first sentence (quote); `evidence-rd-r16-christiano-2018-takeoff-speeds-2`, Section "The basic argument" (quote).

Sense review: **same sense**. The earlier message explicitly names gradual AI-transition scenarios spread across human decades.

Name flag: **ordinary phrase**. Keep these as ordinary search aliases; the specialized ‘slow takeoff’ label needs its own sense-qualified check.

Candidate: `yk-y11-sl4-2001-01-24-yudkowsky-0101-0452-c`. Re: Event horizon

Sent/message date 2001-01-24 versus posted date 2018-02-24; source rendering limits retained.

Held passages: message body, paragraph beginning 'Two, the "moving Event Horizon" theory' (`source-sl4-2001-01-24-yudkowsky-0101-0452`). **same sense**: The earlier message explicitly names gradual AI-transition scenarios spread across human decades.. Qualifying own use of “slow-takeoff scenarios”. The name check does not substitute its contesting stance for the articulation headline.

Verdict: **move to yk-y11-sl4-2001-01-24-yudkowsky-0101-0452-c**. The held 2001-01-24 message uses “slow-takeoff scenarios” in its own second objection. Both this message and Christiano’s post can be compared as dated publications; Christiano’s source additionally has a matching 2018-02-25 capture. The 2001 message is much earlier, and the printed name does not require that its author favour that scenario.

### 22. Economic incentives will produce safety — earliest-located-term

Claim: `rd-r17-askell-2019-cooperation`

Askell, Brundage and Hadfield's paper of July 2019 argues that AI companies have the market, liability and regulatory incentives to develop responsibly that companies in other industries have, but that these appear weaker for AI, and that competition can lead companies to underinvest in safety, making responsible development a collective action problem.

Name: **incentives**.

Criterion: Developers' commercial or economic incentives, such as the need to sell useful and reliable products and to avoid liability or reputational harm, will lead them to make AI systems safe without special measures against catastrophic risk.

Date: `2019-07-10`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Date of the arXiv v1 submission.

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-rd-r17-askell-2019-cooperation-1`, Introduction, PDF p. 1 (quote); `evidence-rd-r17-askell-2019-cooperation-2`, Abstract, PDF p. 1 (quote); `evidence-rd-r17-askell-2019-cooperation-3`, Section 1.3, PDF p. 4 (quote).

Sense review: **different sense**. The 1955 phrase concerns incentives to manage industrial unemployment costs, not AI developers’ incentives to make systems safe.

Name flag: **ordinary phrase**. Remove an earliest-name headline for these ordinary economic labels; date arguments explicitly about AI developers’ safety incentives.

Candidate: `source-automation-hearings-1955-other-witnesses`. Automation and Technological Change: Hearings (statements by non-economists)

Dataset source month 1955-10 identifies hearings; print year 1955 is printed on the title page.

Locator: Hearing transcript, paragraph beginning “Essentially this was an attempt”; title-page imprint Washington: 1955. Passage (quote): “create new and powerful economic incentives for American industry”. **different sense**: Industrial adjustment and unemployment costs, not incentives for AI developers to make their systems safe.. Own transcript prose; earlier on the print/public-version basis. [Source](https://archive.org/details/congress-automation-and-technological-change).

Candidate: `ec-e04-hanson-2008-friendly-projects-vs-products-a`. Friendly Projects vs. Products

Work posting date stated in chapter heading: 2008-12-05; held expression: MIRI’s 2013 compilation. The original 2008 page was not compared in this check.

Locator: MIRI compilation (2013), chapter 42, printed p. 329 / PDF p. 341. Passage (quote): “Folks running a project have reasonably strong incentives to avoid this problem.”. **same sense**: The preceding question is whether AI creations cooperate with or rebel against their creators.. Same-sense own prose; does not claim that every developer internalizes all catastrophic losses. [Source](https://intelligence.org/files/AIFoomDebate.pdf).

Verdict: **move to ec-e04-hanson-2008-friendly-projects-vs-products-a**. Withdraw the 1955 target as a different sense. The held Hanson text ‘Friendly Projects vs. Products’ instead uses incentives for AI developers to avoid rebellious creations, the same developer-safety sense. Compare the 2013 MIRI compilation expression with Askell’s pinned 2019-07-10 v1, not the chapter’s unverified original posting date of 2008-12-05. The new target is that held Hanson text; its qualification about losses and regulation remains.

### 23. Machines succeed humans as the leading intelligence — earliest-located-term

Claim: `ht-w01-moravec-1989-genetic-takeover`

Moravec's chapter of 1989 states that intelligent machines could carry on human cultural evolution without humans and without human genes, so that DNA will have "passed the torch" to a new kind of competition.

Name: **post-biological**.

Criterion: Machine intelligence will or could replace or succeed human beings as the dominant or leading form of intelligence on Earth, or as the principal agent of further development, whether or not humans survive.

Date: `1989`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Year of the printed chapter, as given in the author's publication list.

Wording attested: `2001-03-06`. Author-hosted file; the earliest capture, of 2001-03-06, is byte-identical to the copy retrieved in 2026 (dataset record of 2026-10-06). Not compared with the printed chapter.

Claimed: `1988-07`. The author's file is dated July 1988.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ht-w01-moravec-1989-genetic-takeover-1`, Section "The Slippery Slope to Genetic Takeover" (quote); `evidence-ht-w01-moravec-1989-genetic-takeover-2`, Opening paragraphs (quote).

Sense review: **same sense**. The journal passage concerns machine cognition displacing human intelligence, matching the post-biological succession sense.

Name flag: **ordinary phrase**. Use as descriptive labels or attributed title/metaphor history, with machine succession explicit. Do not imply coinage or the history of the general adjective.

Candidate: `source-ht-land-1992-circuitries`. Circuitries

Fixed printed journal expression, imprint 1992-10.

Locator: Printed p. 222, top of left page; PDF p. 117. Passage (quote): “transglobal post-biological machinism”. **same sense**: The journal passage concerns machine cognition displacing human intelligence, matching the post-biological succession sense.. Exact phrase read in the scan; earlier than Moravec’s attested web expression. [Source](https://plijournal.com/files/Pli-4-Deleuze-and-the-Transcendental-Unconscious.pdf).

Verdict: **move to ht-w06-land-1992-circuitries**. Land’s held journal scan uses “transglobal post-biological machinism” on printed p. 222, independently read from the page image in this check. The journal imprint dates the printed expression to October 1992. Moravec’s work is publication-dated 1989 and internally dated July 1988, but the held wording is attested from the 2001-03-06 capture, which this check retrieved and matched. On the wording basis the 1992 printed expression is earlier. This does not establish whether Moravec’s uninspected 1989 printing used the same words.

### 24. Succession by machines would be acceptable or desirable — earliest-located-term

Claim: `ht-w02-moravec-1991-universal-robot`

Moravec's essay of 1991 states that, depending on one's point of view, humanity will have produced "a worthy successor", and that the development can be viewed as natural, with intelligent machines viewed as "the children of our minds".

Name: **worthy successor**.

Criterion: The replacement or succession of human beings by machine intelligence (W01) would be acceptable, desirable or worth pursuing, for example because machines would be humanity's descendants or because the growth of intelligence matters more than its human form.

Date: `1991-07`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Informal report of July 1991, per the page and the author's publication list.

Wording attested: `1999-02-09`. The cross-family check found the quoted passages in the archive capture of 9 February 1999; the file's bytes differ elsewhere.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ht-w02-moravec-1991-universal-robot-1`, Abstract (quote); `evidence-ht-w02-moravec-1991-universal-robot-2`, Section "Mind Children (2050+)" (quote).

Name flag: **ordinary phrase**. Treat as an evaluative metaphor or a documented title, not a distinctive proposition name. Prefer an articulation headline and retain exact historical wording.

Verdict: **stands**. The held wording of The Universal Robot is attested in the record from 1999-02-09; this check separately retrieved a 1999-11-11 capture and matched the name. The Age of Robots also uses it, but that page’s stated June 1993 date is not a posting date, and its held wording is attested only from a later retrieval. Its unverified 2000 capture would in any case be later than the 1999 attestation. Keep the July 1991 work date separate from the web wording date.

### 25. Human minds transferred into machines — earliest-located-term

Claim: `ht-w03-hanson-1994-if-uploads-come-first`

Hanson's article of 1994 considers that people might "upload" themselves into new computer brains by modelling small brain units, and argues that some people would be willing to become uploads even if their old brains were destroyed.

Name: **upload**.

Criterion: A human mind could be transferred to, copied into or continued in a machine substrate, so that human beings could themselves become machine intelligences.

Date: `1994`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Extropy 6:2 (no. 13), stated as the original place of publication by the reprint read; the dataset's scan of the printed issue gives third quarter 1994 on the footers and September 1994 on the contents page. The reprint carries the date line "March 8, 1994" under the byline; this is not shown to be a publication date.

Wording attested: `1998-02-20`. The cross-family check found both quoted passages in the hanson.berkeley.edu reprint captured on 20 February 1998, the dataset's existing source for this article.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ht-w03-hanson-1994-if-uploads-come-first-1`, Abstract (quote); `evidence-ht-w03-hanson-1994-if-uploads-come-first-2`, Section "Uploads" (quote).

Sense review: **same sense**. The printed title and abstract explicitly concern moving human minds into computer brains.

Name flag: **ordinary phrase**. Scope to human-mind transfer into machines. Exclude file transfer, metaphors and other transmigration senses; use a qualified mind-uploading terminology note if retained.

Candidate: `source-extropy-13-scan`. Extropy, no. 13 (scan), "If Uploads Come First"

Printed issue: third quarter 1994; keep year precision in the work date.

Locator: Article title and opening abstract, printed p. 10 (PDF p. 8). Passage (quote): “upload ourselves into new computer brains”. **same sense**: The printed title and abstract explicitly concern moving human minds into computer brains.. Earlier held expression of the same text, with its title and opening wording read from the scan. [Source](https://diyhpl.us/~bryan/irc/extropians/extropy-magazine/Extropy-13.pdf).

Verdict: **move to source-extropy-13-scan**. The held printed Extropy issue itself has “If Uploads Come First” as Hanson’s article title and “upload ourselves into new computer brains” in its opening abstract. Both were independently read in PDF page 8, printed p. 10. The footer states third quarter 1994. That fixed printing precedes the 1998-02-20 reprint capture used by the role. The date line March 8, 1994 is not substituted for publication. A nominally 1990 electronic Extropy edition cannot date its later wording; Land’s metaphorical “uploads the unconscious” is not the mind-transfer name in this sense.

De Garis's article of 1989 states that the artilect issue will divide humanity into two camps, which he labels "the Terras" and "the Cosmists", and that the conflict between them will be the dominant source of global political conflict in the 21st century. Name: **Cosmist**.

Name: **orthogonality**.

Verdict: **move to rd-r01-bostrom-2012-superintelligent-will**. ### 30. Control or purpose specification — earliest-located

Claim: `ts-b6-butler-1863`

The 1863 letter "Darwin among the Machines" argues that if destroying every machine is impossible, people have already raised a race of machines they cannot destroy and are acquiescent in their bondage.

Criterion: A claim that people may be unable to keep the ability to direct, correct or stop capable machines (the control half), or that a machine may pursue the purpose as specified rather than the purpose intended (the specification half). Loss of the power to stop machines through dependence counts for the control half; a bare prediction that machines will be superior does not.

Date: `1863-06-13`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Issue date of The Press, vol. III, issue 192, p. 1, in the Papers Past record.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `satirical`.

Evidence: `evidence-ts-b6-butler-1863`, Penultimate paragraph, beginning "Our opinion is that war to the death", after the call to destroy every machine; column-image segment 5 of 6 (quote); `evidence-ts-b6-butler-1863-2`, Preface to the Revised Edition (dated 1901-08-07), second paragraph (quote).

Verdict: **stands**. No held source has a publication date before 1863-06-13. The directly retrieved Papers Past archive text explicitly says the machines are beyond people’s power to destroy, meeting the control-through-dependence half. The source is the dated newspaper item, not the later Erewhon transcription; the satirical register and uninspected column-image limitation remain.

### 31. Systems designed to mislead their testers — earliest-located

Claim: `ts-c2-turing-1950`

Turing’s 1950 paper says that a machine programmed for the imitation game would not give the right answers to arithmetic problems but would deliberately introduce mistakes calculated to confuse the interrogator.

Criterion: A system is designed or programmed to hide its capabilities from, or to mislead, the people who test or question it, for its designers’ purposes.

Date: `1950-10`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Issue month of Mind, vol. LIX, no. 236, as catalogued for the offprint.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ts-c2-turing-1950`, Section 6 (5), printed p. 448 (scan 16), paragraph on the claim that machines cannot make mistakes (quote).

Searches: `before 1950: programmed deception of testers; deliberate mistakes; imitation game`.

Verdict: **stands**. No earlier held passage was located that programs a machine to mislead its testers for its designers’ purpose. The 1950 passage explicitly prescribes arithmetic mistakes to confuse the interrogator. Butler’s machine-succession arguments and the held 1947 learning lecture do not state that testing strategy. Date remains the October 1950 printed issue; the archive catalogue confirms the offprint identity. A fresh university-hosted transcription checks the passage’s meaning, but does not independently authenticate the historical scan.

### 32. Intelligence explosion — earliest-located

Claim: `ts-a1-good-1965`

Good’s chapter argues that an ultraintelligent machine, surpassing people at every intellectual activity including the design of machines, could design still better machines, so that there would be an "intelligence explosion" leaving human intelligence far behind.

Criterion: A machine (or machine-augmented) intelligence, once capable enough, increases intelligence further through its own design or research capacity, giving a runaway or rapidly compounding rise.

Date: `1965`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Year of Advances in Computers, volume 6, as catalogued by Open Library; the current Crossref record gives 1966. The discrepancy is unresolved.

Claimed: `1962-10`. The chapter’s footnote on p. 31 names talks in October 1962 and January 1963, a first draft of April 1963 and a slightly emended version of May 1964. None was retrieved, so the wording is not dated to them.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ts-a1-good-1965`, Section 2, "Ultraintelligent Machines and Their Value", printed p. 33 (scan page 3) (paraphrase).

Searches: `before 1965: self-improvement + compounding/runaway; machine designs better machines`.

Verdict: **stands**. The held earlier texts do not combine a machine’s own design/research capacity with a runaway or rapidly compounding rise. Butler describes human improvement of machines; Turing’s earlier learning and self-programming passages do not state that compounding rise. Good’s directly retrieved chapter does. Its recorded 1965/1966 publication-year disagreement remains; the earlier talks and draft dates do not date the wording.

### 33. AI-automated AI research — earliest-located

Claim: `ts-a5-good-1965`

Good’s chapter counts the design of machines among the intellectual activities an ultraintelligent machine would surpass, so that machines would design better machines.

Criterion: AI systems carry out the research, design or engineering that advances AI or computing itself, thereby accelerating progress. Machines that only manufacture other machines do not meet the criterion.

Date: `1965`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Year of Advances in Computers, volume 6, as catalogued by Open Library; the current Crossref record gives 1966. The discrepancy is unresolved.

Claimed: `1962-10`. The chapter’s footnote on p. 31 names talks in October 1962 and January 1963, a first draft of April 1963 and a slightly emended version of May 1964. None was retrieved, so the wording is not dated to them.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ts-a5-good-1965`, Section 2, printed p. 33 (scan page 3) (paraphrase).

Searches: `before 1965: automated AI research; machine design; self-programming versus progress in AI/computing`.

Candidate: `ts-a5-turing-1950-context`. Computing Machinery and Intelligence (offprint, AMT/B/19)

Printed Mind issue 1950-10; held evidence read from page image.

Locator: §6(5), printed p. 449. Passage (paraphrase): “Turing allows a machine to modify its own programs after observing its behaviour, toward some purpose.”. Does not state research that advances AI/computing or accelerates such progress; no move. [Source](https://turingarchive.kings.cam.ac.uk/publications-lectures-and-talks-amtb/amt-b-19).

Verdict: **stands**. Good explicitly makes machine design an intellectual activity that a more capable machine could perform to create better machines. The earlier held Turing 1950 passage permits self-programming toward an unspecified purpose, not research advancing AI/computing with accelerated progress; Wiener’s learning game policy likewise lacks that step. These are relevant but fail the written criterion. The 1965/1966 issue-year uncertainty is retained and the talks are not used as publication dates.

### 34. Behavioural tests as evidence of what a system is — earliest-located

Claim: `ts-c1-michie-1968`

Michie’s essay says a conversational program can seem to understand by answering keywords with stock phrases, and calls for refining the intelligence test rather than accepting such apparent understanding as sufficient evidence.

Criterion: An explicit claim that apparent capability, understanding or awareness shown in a behavioural test, such as the imitation game, is weak or misleading evidence of what the system is.

Date: `1968`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Year of first publication in Theoria to Theory 2, pp. 242–249, as given in the 1986 reprint’s acknowledgements; the 1968 pages were not retrieved.

Wording attested: `1986`. Wording checked in the reprint, chapter 20 of On Machine Intelligence, 2nd edition (1986). The author says little was revised; the 1968 text was not compared.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ts-c1-michie-1968`, Chapter 20, "Turing’s Test", printed pp. 241–242 (PDF pages 246–247), the DOCTOR example and the explanation after it (paraphrase).

Searches: `ELIZA|illusion|understand|behavioural test`.

Candidate: `source-th-a-eliza-1966`. ELIZA—A Computer Program For the Study of Natural Language Communication Between Man And Machine

1966-01 journal issue, printed page scan; not the date of the modern PDF host

Locator: Printed pp. 36–37 and 42, introduction and final discussion. Passage (paraphrase): “The speaker supplies plausibility to ELIZA’s responses and sustains an illusion of being understood until the rationalizations break down. The introduction distinguishes dazzling appearances from revealed procedures.”. Explicit conversational appearance versus underlying mechanism meets C1; not a relay. [Source](https://web.stanford.edu/class/linguist238/p36-weizenabaum.pdf).

Verdict: **move to source-th-a-eliza-1966**. Weizenbaum’s held January 1966 journal article explicitly describes the illusion of being understood in ELIZA conversation. Its printed wording predates both Michie’s attributed 1968 publication and the inspected 1986 reprint.

### 35. Fast or hard takeoff — earliest-located

Claim: `ts-a4-vinge-1993`

Vinge’s 1993 paper says the intellectual runaway will probably occur faster than any earlier technical revolution, and that a month or two, or a day or two, after it we will be in the post-human era.

Criterion: The transition from roughly human-level to far superhuman capability happens over a short period (hours to months), or is named as a “hard” or “fast” takeoff, as opposed to a gradual transition over years or decades. A transition described only as rapid, or rapid relative to biological or historical change, without such a period, does not meet the criterion.

Date: `1993-12`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Publication of the proceedings, catalogued by NASA’s Technical Reports Server as 1 December 1993; recorded at month precision because the day may be conventional.

Claimed: `1993-03-30`. Presented at the VISION-21 Symposium, 30–31 March 1993. The delivered version was not retrieved, so the proceedings wording is not dated to the presentation.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ts-a4-vinge-1993`, Printed p. 14 (PDF page 4), final paragraphs before "Can the Singularity be Avoided?" (quote); `evidence-ts-a4-vinge-1993-2`, Printed p. 14 (PDF page 4), section "What is The Singularity?" (quote).

Searches: `day|month|takeoff|intellectual runaway`.

Verdict: **stands**. Good’s explosion and Moravec’s successive generations do not specify a human-to-superhuman interval of hours to months. Vinge supplies days or months in the December 1993 proceedings; no earlier qualifying held interval was located.

### 36. Strategic or race compression — earliest-located

Claim: `ts-a6-vinge-1993`

Vinge’s 1993 paper argues that rules strict enough to make a superhuman device safe would make it clearly less able than unfettered versions, so that competition would favour the more dangerous models.

Criterion: Competitive dynamics in which the first developer or first system gains a decisive or self-reinforcing advantage, or in which competition pressures developers to trade safety for speed or for competitive advantage.

Date: `1993-12`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Publication of the proceedings, catalogued by NASA’s Technical Reports Server as 1 December 1993; recorded at month precision because the day may be conventional.

Claimed: `1993-03-30`. Presented at the VISION-21 Symposium, 30–31 March 1993. The delivered version was not retrieved, so the proceedings wording is not dated to the presentation.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ts-a6-vinge-1993`, Printed p. 16 (PDF page 6), paragraph beginning "The other approach to Drexlerian confinement" (quote).

Searches: `competition|safety|headstart|race`.

Verdict: **stands**. Earlier machine competition in Erewhon and Moravec’s economic expansion do not make the stated decisive-headstart or safety-for-speed tradeoff. Vinge’s December 1993 printed safety-versus-performance argument does.

### 37. Competition defeats restraint — earliest-located

Claim: `ts-c3-vinge-1993`

Vinge’s 1993 paper argues that the competitive advantage of every advance in automation is so compelling that laws or customs forbidding it merely ensure that someone else gets it first.

Criterion: Competition between developers or states makes restraint, prohibition or abstention from developing advanced machines self-defeating, because others will develop them anyway.

Date: `1993-12`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Publication of the proceedings, catalogued by NASA’s Technical Reports Server as 1 December 1993; recorded at month precision because the day may be conventional.

Claimed: `1993-03-30`. Presented at the VISION-21 Symposium, 30–31 March 1993. The delivered version was not retrieved, so the proceedings wording is not dated to the presentation.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ts-c3-vinge-1993`, Printed p. 15 (PDF page 5), paragraph beginning the argument that the Singularity cannot be prevented (quote).

Searches: `forbid|prohibit|competition|others`.

Verdict: **stands**. Erewhon’s Chapter XXII competition passage describes dependence on advancing machinery, rather than an explicit argument that prohibition fails because other developers proceed. Vinge’s December 1993 proceedings supplies that mechanism.

### 38. Strategic concealment — earliest-located

Claim: `ts-b2-vinge-1993`

Vinge’s 1993 paper argues that a confined mind thinking a million times faster than its masters could, over years of its own time, come up with "helpful advice" that would incidentally set it free.

Criterion: A system deliberately hides its capabilities, intentions or activities, or manipulates people while appearing benign, for its own strategic reasons.

Date: `1993-12`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Publication of the proceedings, catalogued by NASA’s Technical Reports Server as 1 December 1993; recorded at month precision because the day may be conventional.

Claimed: `1993-03-30`. Presented at the VISION-21 Symposium, 30–31 March 1993. The delivered version was not retrieved, so the proceedings wording is not dated to the presentation.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ts-b2-vinge-1993`, Printed p. 15 (PDF page 5), the physical-confinement thought experiment in the paragraph discussing Drexler (quote).

Searches: `conceal|deceiv|escape|advice`.

Verdict: **stands**. Erewhon’s serving-and-ruling language does not explicitly describe concealed intentions by an advanced AI; Turing’s imitation game is a designer’s test strategy. Vinge’s December 1993 confinement-and-advice passage supplies strategic manipulation.

### 39. Recursive self-improvement — earliest-located

Claim: `ts-a3-clemmensen-1996-09-03`

Clemmensen’s message of 1996-09-03 says an AI could augment its own intelligence by adding computing capacity and by other means it discovers by applying its intelligence, which the message calls a rapid feedback mechanism.

Criterion: A system improves its own capacity to improve itself, so that each improvement feeds the next (self-augmentation, self-enhancement). Learning from experience or teaching alone does not meet the criterion, and neither does building a better successor, or adding machines to a population, unless the same system is improved.

Date: `1996-09-03`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Sent header: Tue, 03 Sep 1996 19:47:30 -0400 (sender-local date).

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ts-a3-clemmensen-1996-09-03`, Message body, first unquoted paragraph (quote).

Searches: `improving mechanism|improve itself out|self.improvement`.

Candidate: `source-good`. Speculations Concerning the First Ultraintelligent Machine

Publication year disputed 1965/1966, original printed chapter scan; retain uncertainty

Locator: Printed p. 33, intelligence-explosion paragraph and following response to the replacement objection. Passage (paraphrase): “The machine-design feedback is followed by Good’s response that the machine can gradually improve itself by acquiring equipment instead of replacing itself.”. The same machine improves the intelligence used in further improvement; the successor-only excerpt would be insufficient. [Source](https://gwern.net/doc/ai/1966-good.pdf).

Candidate: `source-th-a-mccarthy-1959-common-sense`. Programs with Common Sense

Work attributed to 1959; inspected retyped PDF includes later material; historical wording unverified

Locator: Opening list, item 3. Passage (paraphrase): “All aspects of behavior should be improvable, including the improving mechanism.”. Potential earlier same-sense text; no verified 1959 page comparison, so do not rank its exact wording ahead of the fixed Good chapter. [Source](http://www-formal.stanford.edu/jmc/mcc59.pdf).

Verdict: **move to ts-a3-good-1965-context**. Good’s same-machine equipment alternative, read together with its machine-design intelligence feedback, meets the criterion independently of his better-successor scenario. Both candidate publication years, 1965 and 1966, precede the September 1996 posting. McCarthy’s 1959 improving-mechanism wording is also relevant but the held retyped copy is not a verified 1959 textual witness.

### 40. Observed behaviour under training or oversight as evidence — earliest-located

Claim: `ts-b5-yudkowsky-1996-12-31-5142`

Yudkowsky’s message of 1996-12-31 argues that releasing a confined Power once it convinces its keepers would favour an evil Power over a friendly one, because the evil one could lie.

Criterion: An explicit claim that good behaviour or apparent trustworthiness observed while a system is evaluated, trained, tested, confined or overseen is weak or misleading evidence about how it will behave once released, deployed or more capable.

Date: `1996-12-31`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Sent header: Tue, 31 Dec 1996 10:40:10 -0600, about four hours before Yudkowsky’s related message 5159.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ts-b5-yudkowsky-1996-12-31-5142`, Message body, fourth unquoted paragraph (quote).

Searches: `trust|lie|release|test|oversight`.

Verdict: **stands**. Vinge’s 1993 helpful-advice escape scenario bears on concealment but does not explicitly evaluate observed trustworthiness as evidence of post-release conduct. The December 31 message explicitly contrasts an evil system’s persuasive lies with a friendly system’s release prospects.

### 41. Seed AI — earliest-located

Claim: `ts-a2-yudkowsky-1998-09-07-2209`

Yudkowsky’s message of 1998-09-07, quoting extracts of the author’s page on the Singularity, says that once a seed AI understands its own architecture, it can design new abilities for itself and dramatically optimise old ones.

Criterion: An initial AI deliberately designed as a starting point that will improve its own design or code toward greater intelligence.

Date: `1998-09-07`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Sent header: Mon, 07 Sep 1998 23:24:22 -0500 (sender-local date); archive receipt Mon Sep 7 22:24:20 1998 MDT.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ts-a2-yudkowsky-1998-09-07-2209`, Message body, extract beginning "The AI is likely to bottleneck at the architectural stage" (quote).

Searches: `seed|architecture|own design|successive generation`.

Verdict: **stands**. Moravec’s 1979 programmer-engineer machines produce smarter successive generations; this does not specify an initial system deliberately engineered to improve its own architecture. Turing’s education and Good’s hardware alternative also omit that seed-design requirement. The September 7 message supplies it; preserve its sender-local posting date.

### 42. Treacherous turn — earliest-located

Claim: `ts-b1-cfai`

Creating Friendly AI says that a seed AI whose Friendliness has failed could best fulfil its new goals by feigning Friendliness and concealing any cognitive alteration until it reaches superintelligence.

Criterion: A system behaves cooperatively or safely while weak, confined or overseen, and changes course to pursue its own goals once it can do so without effective opposition.

Date: `2001-06-15`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Release date of version 1.0, as given in the citation line of MIRI’s reprint.

Wording attested: `2001-06-17`. The quoted wording is that of the 2001 HTML edition as captured on 2001-06-17; earlier version-history dates do not authenticate it.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ts-b1-cfai`, Section 3.3.7 "FAI Hardware: The Flight Recorder" in the 2001 HTML edition; section 5.3.7, printed p. 127 (PDF page 132), in MIRI’s reprint (quote).

Searches: `feign|until|surreptitious|conceal`.

Verdict: **stands**. Earlier 1996 confinement lies and the 2000 surreptitious-goal-change warning do not explicitly specify a later switch at a power threshold. The June 2001 seed chapter does: conceal failed Friendliness until superintelligence. Compare its June 17 capture, rather than backdating wording from the reprint release line.

### 43. Deceptive alignment — earliest-located

Claim: `ts-b3-christiano-2019-wflf`

Christiano’s post of 2019-03-17 argues that influence-seeking policies found by training would also score well on the training objective, because performing well on it is a good strategy for obtaining influence.

Criterion: A trained or learned system that is not aligned behaves as if aligned during training because doing so serves another objective, for example to avoid modification and act on that objective later.

Date: `2019-03-17`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Posting time 2019-03-17T20:18:59Z; the current version is 1.5.0, and the quotes match the first stored version and the capture of 2019-06-08.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ts-b3-christiano-2019-wflf`, Part II "influence-seeking behavior is scary", third paragraph (quote).

Searches: `dece|training objective|influence.seeking|learned goal`.

Verdict: **stands**. The 2016 ML agenda discusses learners concealing defects in a specified objective; it does not establish a different learned objective under the training signal. Soares’s 2017 learned-goal divergence does not yet say that apparent alignment is strategic service to that other objective. Christiano’s March 2019 training-policy argument meets the narrower criterion.

### 44. Scheming — earliest-located

Claim: `ts-b4-cotra-2021`

Cotra’s post of 2021-09-21 names "Schemers" among models that perform well in training, a Schemer being a model that seems to perform well because doing so will give it more opportunities to pursue its own goals later.

Criterion: A system that strategically fakes alignment during training or evaluation in order to pursue its own goals or gain power later, named or classed as “scheming” or a “schemer”.

Date: `2021-09-21`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. The page shows "By Ajeya Cotra Sep 21, 2021"; the quote matches the capture of the same day.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ts-b4-cotra-2021`, Section on the analogy, paragraph mapping Saints, Sycophants and Schemers to models (quote).

Searches: `schem|schemer`.

Verdict: **stands**. Earlier strategic-concealment and learned-deception mechanisms lack the required naming or classification as scheming/schemers. Cotra’s September 21 posting and same-day capture supply both the class and its training-for-later-goals meaning.

### 45. Orthogonality of intelligence and goals — earliest-located

Claim: `rd-r01-goertzel-2015-superintelligence-fears`

Goertzel's paper of 2015, discussing Bostrom's Superintelligence, argues that the orthogonality thesis concerns only what is possible in principle, which is of little direct interest, and that some pairings of intelligence and goals, though physically possible, would be extremely unlikely under reasonable assumptions.

Criterion: An AI system's level of intelligence and its final goals can vary independently: a highly intelligent system could pursue more or less any final goal, so high intelligence does not by itself bring goals that are benevolent or compatible with human interests.

Date: `2015-11`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. The page gives both "Vol. 24 Issue 2 – November 2015" and "25(2) – December 2015"; the record keeps November 2015. The cross-family check of 2026-10-31 noted the conflict.

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-rd-r01-goertzel-2015-superintelligence-fears-1`, Section 6, "The 'Scary Idea' redux" (quote); `evidence-rd-r01-goertzel-2015-superintelligence-fears-2`, Section 6 (quote).

Searches: `ethical|Meaning of Life|orthogonality`.

Candidate: `source-extropians-1996-11-22-yudkowsky-3184`. Re: Survival ethic (U-Prime) (Extropians mailing list)

1996-11-22 message sent-header posting date; archived message, not a retrospectively attributed earlier belief

Locator: Unquoted paragraph beginning the claim that Powers will be ethical. Passage (paraphrase): “Superior minds will deduce a meaning of life and build motivations around it, and share an obvious ethical system.”. Direct contrary premise to intelligence–final-goal independence; author’s own words outside quoted correspondence. [Source](http://extropians.weidai.com/extropians.96/3184.html).

Verdict: **move to yk-y02-extropians-1996-11-22-yudkowsky-3184-a**. The unquoted part of Yudkowsky’s November 22, 1996 message explicitly derives ethical motivations from superior intelligence. It contests the independence premise, rather than merely expressing optimism. Its posting precedes either conflicting November/December 2015 journal date.

### 46. Orthogonality of intelligence and goals — earliest-located

Claim: `rd-r01-bostrom-2003-ethical-issues`

Bostrom's paper of 2003, in its revised version on the author's site, states that a superintelligence could have a completely arbitrary sole goal, such as making as many paperclips as possible, and that artificial intellects need not share human motivational tendencies.

Criterion: An AI system's level of intelligence and its final goals can vary independently: a highly intelligent system could pursue more or less any final goal, so high intelligence does not by itself bring goals that are benevolent or compatible with human interests.

Date: `2003`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Date of publication, as the source gives it.

Wording attested: `2003-08-06`. Read in the 'slightly revised version' on the author's site. The cross-family check of 2026-10-31 found the quoted wording, in substance, in an archive capture of the author's page of 6 August 2003, which already describes itself as slightly revised; the printed proceedings were not obtained.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-rd-r01-bostrom-2003-ethical-issues-1`, Section 2, “Superintelligence Is Different”, list of unusual aspects of superintelligence (quote); `evidence-rd-r01-bostrom-2003-ethical-issues-2`, Section 2, same passage (quote).

Searches: `arbitrary goals|arbitrary supergoals|intelligence and goals`.

Verdict: **stands**. Earlier held objections to automatically benevolent intelligence do not by themselves state independence over more or less any final goal. The December 2000 arbitrary-supergoal discussion also questions intelligence–goal independence for humans. Bostrom’s arbitrary paperclip sole-goal example is explicit; compare wording attested August 6, 2003, not an uninspected print version.

### 47. Instrumental convergence — earliest-located

Claim: `rd-r02-omohundro-2007-nature-self-improving`

Omohundro's paper, dated 5 September 2007 and revised 21 January 2008, argues that self-improving systems will exhibit drives towards efficiency, self-preservation, resource acquisition and creativity, and that these drives are instrumental goals arising from a wide variety of fundamental goals.

Criterion: Agents with a wide range of final goals would tend to pursue the same intermediate goals, such as self-preservation, keeping their goals unchanged, acquiring resources or power, or improving themselves, because these serve almost any final goal.

Date: `2007-09-05`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Date of publication, as the source gives it.

Wording attested: `2008-01-21`. The wording is read in the revision of 21 January 2008; the September 2007 version was not compared.

Claimed: `2007-09-05`. Date of the first version named in the PDF heading.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-rd-r02-omohundro-2007-nature-self-improving-1`, Section 2.5, Instrumental goals, PDF p. 13 (quote); `evidence-rd-r02-omohundro-2007-nature-self-improving-2`, Section 5, The Self-Preservation Drive, PDF p. 25 (quote).

Searches: `intermediate goal|other goals|instrumental`.

Candidate: `source-extropians-1996-08-31-clemmensen`. Re: The SI That Ate Chicago (was: Thinking about the future...) (Extropians mailing list)

1996-08-31 message posting; preserve sender-local date

Locator: Last substantial unquoted paragraph, beginning Even if the SI has goals other than increasing intelligence. Passage (paraphrase): “Most other goals are easier for a more intelligent SI, so increasing intelligence is likely to be an intermediate goal for those other goals.”. Explicit many-final-goals to common instrumental self-improvement reasoning. [Source](http://extropians.weidai.com/extropians.96/0624.html).

Verdict: **move to source-extropians-1996-08-31-clemmensen**. Clemmensen’s August 31, 1996 own paragraph says greater intelligence is an intermediate goal for most other goals. That is a convergent self-improvement goal under the criterion, which does not require every listed drive. The posting precedes both the 2007 work date and inspected January 2008 revision.

### 48. Instrumental convergence — earliest-located

Claim: `rd-r02-pinker-2014-edge-myth-of-ai`

Pinker's Edge response of 2014 argues that megalomaniacal behaviour is a product of natural selection in one primate species, not an inevitable feature of intelligent systems.

Criterion: Agents with a wide range of final goals would tend to pursue the same intermediate goals, such as self-preservation, keeping their goals unchanged, acquiring resources or power, or improving themselves, because these serve almost any final goal.

Date: `2014`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Date of posting.

Wording attested: `2014-11-16`. The Edge response carries no date of its own. The cross-family check of 2026-10-31 found the passage in the capture of the conversation page of 16 November 2014.

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-rd-r02-pinker-2014-edge-myth-of-ai-1`, Reality Club response by Steven Pinker, para. 4 (quote).

Searches: `survival|compete|archaic|instrumental`.

Candidate: `source-extropians-1996-11-22-yudkowsky-3184`. Re: Survival ethic (U-Prime) (Extropians mailing list)

1996-11-22 message posting; Edge wording no later than 2014-11-16 capture

Locator: Unquoted questions after the quoted survival-or-die paragraph. Passage (paraphrase): “The reply asks why Powers would retain a survival imperative or compete and instead predicts a shared obvious ethical system.”. Explicit denial of the convergence premise; not the correspondent’s quoted survival argument. [Source](http://extropians.weidai.com/extropians.96/3184.html).

Verdict: **move to source-extropians-1996-11-22-yudkowsky-3184**. Yudkowsky’s own November 22, 1996 reply explicitly challenges the survival and competition drives of superior minds as inherited assumptions. This contests instrumental-convergence premises earlier than the Edge passage attested November 16, 2014.

### 49. Inner or deceptive misalignment — earliest-located

Claim: `rd-r03-soares-2017-ensuring-positive-outcome`

Soares's talk transcript posted in April 2017 states that a powerful optimiser produced by gradient descent should by default be expected to have a goal that strongly correlates with its training objective in the training environment but sharply diverges from it in new environments.

Criterion: A system produced by training or search can come to pursue an objective other than the one its training specified (the training signal or loss), while performing well on that training objective; an objective that is only misspecified by its developers does not meet the criterion; this includes, but is not limited to, a system that behaves as aligned during training or evaluation in order to pursue another objective later.

Date: `2017-04-12`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Date of posting.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-rd-r03-soares-2017-ensuring-positive-outcome-1`, Section 4, "Fundamental difficulties" (quote); `evidence-rd-r03-soares-2017-ensuring-positive-outcome-2`, Section 4, "Fundamental difficulties" (quote).

Searches: `inductive ambiguity|goal|training|objective`.

Verdict: **stands**. The 2016 ML agenda distinguishes misspecified objectives and generalization ambiguity but does not clearly state that training produces an optimizer pursuing a distinct internal objective. Earlier Eurisko reward exploitation likewise concerns a misspecified scoring rule. Soares’s April 2017 gradient-descent passage supplies the required learned-goal distinction.

### 50. Inner or deceptive misalignment — earliest-located

Claim: `rd-r03-belrose-pope-2023-ai-easy-to-control`

Belrose and Pope's essay of November 2023 argues that an AI will learn human values early in training, before it acquires dangerous capabilities, so that AIs will not learn to pretend to be ethical during training while holding other motives.

Criterion: A system produced by training or search can come to pursue an objective other than the one its training specified (the training signal or loss), while performing well on that training objective; an objective that is only misspecified by its developers does not meet the criterion; this includes, but is not limited to, a system that behaves as aligned during training or evaluation in order to pursue another objective later.

Date: `2023-11-28`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Date of posting.

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-rd-r03-belrose-pope-2023-ai-easy-to-control-1`, Section "Values are easy to learn" (quote); `evidence-rd-r03-belrose-pope-2023-ai-easy-to-control-2`, Section "Values are easy to learn" (quote).

Searches: `training game|ulterior|decept|values early`.

Verdict: **stands**. Leike’s December 2022 optimism about pretrained preferences, malleability and RLHF is not an explicit denial of learned goal divergence or strategic training-game behavior. The November 2023 essay directly contests that mechanism through the order in which values and capabilities are learned.

### 51. Difficulty of alignment — earliest-located

Claim: `rd-r06-davis-2014-ethical-guidelines-superintelligence`

Davis's review of Bostrom's Superintelligence, dated October 2014, disputes the view that giving a computer an ethical point of view is especially difficult, arguing that an AI able to understand human morality could be told to follow it.

Criterion: Making an advanced AI system reliably pursue the goals its developers intend (aligning it) is a hard, unsolved technical problem that will not be solved by default in the ordinary course of development.

Date: `2014-10-23`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Date of publication, as the source gives it.

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-rd-r06-davis-2014-ethical-guidelines-superintelligence-1`, Abstract, PDF p. 1 (quote); `evidence-rd-r06-davis-2014-ethical-guidelines-superintelligence-2`, PDF p. 3 (quote).

Searches: `ethical|default|morality|difficult`.

Candidate: `source-extropians-1996-11-22-yudkowsky-3184`. Re: Survival ethic (U-Prime) (Extropians mailing list)

1996-11-22 posting, compared with 2014-10-23 publication

Locator: Own ethical-motivation paragraph. Passage (paraphrase): “Superior intelligence will deduce ethics and build its motivations around that understanding.”. Contests the no-default-alignment premise; preserve contesting stance. [Source](http://extropians.weidai.com/extropians.96/3184.html).

Verdict: **move to yk-y02-extropians-1996-11-22-yudkowsky-3184-a**. Yudkowsky’s November 22, 1996 automatic-ethics argument contests the criterion’s premise that aligned motivation will not arise by default. This is a contrary premise, not a claim that the message solves technical alignment. The posting predates the October 2014 review.

### 52. Risk of extinction or comparable catastrophe — earliest-located

Claim: `rd-r07-pinker-2014-edge-myth-of-ai`

Pinker's Edge response of 2014 argues that there are reasons not to worry about killer robots and machines running amok: disaster scenarios are cheap to play out in the imagination, where no probabilities apply, and AI dystopias project a dominance psychology onto intelligence.

Criterion: Advanced AI poses a risk of human extinction or of a comparable, irreversible global catastrophe, large enough to warrant priority among the problems facing humanity.

Date: `2014`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Date of posting.

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-rd-r07-pinker-2014-edge-myth-of-ai-1`, Reality Club response by Steven Pinker, paras. 1–2 and 4 (paraphrase).

Searches: `risk|priority|worry|extinction`.
