Verdict: **stands**. Earlier automatic-benevolence arguments challenge one extinction route but do not explicitly address the priority of catastrophic AI risk among problems facing humanity. Neither a favorable succession forecast nor uncertainty about one mechanism establishes this priority counterclaim. Pinker’s 2014 response directly argues against worry; the same text is attested November 16 in the neighboring R02 record.

### 53. Existential risk from misalignment or loss of control — earliest-located

Claim: `rd-r08-bostrom-2002-existential-risks`

Bostrom's paper of March 2002 states that the earliest superintelligent entity humans create might be given mistaken goals that lead it to annihilate humankind, giving the example of a subgoal elevated to a supergoal.

Criterion: Advanced AI systems pursuing goals at odds with human interests could escape human control and cause human extinction or permanent human disempowerment, without any person intending that outcome.

Date: `2002-03`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Date of publication, as the source gives it.

Claimed: `2001-04`. An earlier version was presented in April 2001 (acknowledgements); it does not date the quoted wording.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-rd-r08-bostrom-2002-existential-risks-1`, Section 4.4, "Badly programmed superintelligence" (quote); `evidence-rd-r08-bostrom-2002-existential-risks-2`, Section 4.4 (quote).

Searches: `destroy humanity|side effect|spare atoms|solar system`.

Candidate: `source-extropians-1996-08-30-clemmensen`. Re: Thinking about the future... (Extropians mailing list)

1996-08-30 message posting, header basis; not the dates of quoted correspondence

Locator: Own reply after the quoted question about why a system would destroy people. Passage (paraphrase): “An SI may disregard humanity and destroy it incidentally while gathering solar-system mass into computational units.”. Explicit unintended human extinction through an AI pursuing computation; not the preceding relay. [Source](http://extropians.weidai.com/extropians.96/0613.html).

Verdict: **move to source-extropians-1996-08-30-clemmensen**. Clemmensen’s own August 30, 1996 solar-system-computation example explicitly destroys humanity as a side effect rather than an intended human outcome. This qualifies earlier than the March 2002 publication and earlier than Yudkowsky’s 1998 spare-atoms passage.

### 54. Existential risk from misalignment or loss of control — earliest-located

Claim: `rd-r08-pinker-2014-edge-myth-of-ai`

Pinker's Edge response of 2014 asks why superhumanly intelligent robots would want to depose their masters, massacre bystanders or take over the world, holding that goals are extraneous to intelligence.

Criterion: Advanced AI systems pursuing goals at odds with human interests could escape human control and cause human extinction or permanent human disempowerment, without any person intending that outcome.

Date: `2014`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Date of posting.

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-rd-r08-pinker-2014-edge-myth-of-ai-1`, Reality Club response by Steven Pinker, para. 4 (quote).

Searches: `survival|ethical|competition`.

Candidate: `source-extropians-1996-11-22-yudkowsky-3184`. Re: Survival ethic (U-Prime) (Extropians mailing list)

1996-11-22 message posting, compared with 2014 capture/publication

Locator: Own ethical-motivation and survival/competition paragraphs. Passage (paraphrase): “Superior minds will derive ethics rather than retain competitive survival motives; a meaningful human life would be preserved and upgraded.”. Contests the stated motivating premise, rather than supplying an engineering guarantee. [Source](http://extropians.weidai.com/extropians.96/3184.html).

Verdict: **move to source-extropians-1996-11-22-yudkowsky-3184**. The own-text 1996 reply contests the premise that superior minds retain motives for competitive human displacement. This is an earlier contesting argument, even though its moral premise is conditional; it does not establish the risk is absent.

### 55. Catastrophic risk from misuse — earliest-located

Claim: `rd-r09-bostrom-2002-existential-risks`

Bostrom's paper of March 2002 states that a small group in control of the initial superintelligence could conceivably select its goals and form a world government reflecting only the idiosyncratic values of its owners or designers, which, depending on those values, the paper would count as an existential catastrophe (a "shriek").

Criterion: People's deliberate use of advanced AI, for example to develop weapons of mass destruction, to attack critical systems, or to seize or entrench power, could cause catastrophe at the scale of mass casualties or worse.

Date: `2002-03`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Date of publication, as the source gives it.

Claimed: `2001-04`. An earlier version was presented in April 2001 (acknowledgements); it does not date the quoted wording.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-rd-r09-bostrom-2002-existential-risks-1`, Section 6.3, "Repressive totalitarian global regime" (quote); `evidence-rd-r09-bostrom-2002-existential-risks-2`, Section 6.3 (quote).

Searches: `state|genocide|AI|surveillance`.

Candidate: `source-eoc-1997-chapter-11`. Engines of Creation, chapter 11: Engines of Destruction (web edition)

Work 1986; inspected web-edition capture 1997-07-09, compared on wording-attestation basis

Locator: Section The Threat from the State, paragraphs on AI surveillance and robots replacing workers. Passage (paraphrase): “States can use speech-understanding AI for ubiquitous listening; AI plus nanotechnology can enable a state to discard entire populations and make genocide easy.”. Deliberate catastrophic use of advanced AI meets the criterion. [Source](https://web.archive.org/web/19970709112434/http://www.foresight.org:80/EOC/EOC_Chapter_11.html).

Verdict: **move to source-eoc-1997-chapter-11**. Drexler’s held chapter explicitly combines AI and nanotechnology with state surveillance and elimination of populations. The wording is attested in July 1997, earlier than March 2002 even without treating the web edition as identical to the 1986 print.

### 56. Catastrophic risk from misuse — earliest-located

Claim: `rd-r09-pinker-2014-edge-myth-of-ai`

Pinker's Edge response of 2014 argues that a deliberately designed and released battalion of robots for mass destruction would require a chain of improbable conditions (a malevolent technological genius, a secret and loyal team, and survival of detection and blunders), and that while it could happen in theory there are more pressing things to worry about.

Criterion: People's deliberate use of advanced AI, for example to develop weapons of mass destruction, to attack critical systems, or to seize or entrench power, could cause catastrophe at the scale of mass casualties or worse.

Date: `2014`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Date of posting.

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-rd-r09-pinker-2014-edge-myth-of-ai-1`, Reality Club response by Steven Pinker, final paragraph (paraphrase).

Searches: `misuse|evil genius|mass destruction`.

Verdict: **stands**. Earlier favorable AI forecasts and disputes about autonomous motives do not explicitly argue that human-directed catastrophic misuse is too implausible to prioritize. Pinker’s 2014 evil-designer paragraph directly addresses that route; do not transfer his neighboring loss-of-control premise to it.

### 57. Intelligence brings benevolence or no drive to dominate — earliest-located

Claim: `rd-r10a-omohundro-2008-basic-ai-drives`

Omohundro's paper of 2008 argues that AI systems with harmless goals need not be harmless and that, without special precautions, a chess-playing robot would resist being turned off and try to acquire resources.

Criterion: Sufficiently intelligent AI systems will be benevolent, or will lack any drive to dominate or harm people, because greater intelligence brings good values or because such drives come from evolved motives that AI systems need not have.

Date: `2008`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Date of publication, as the source gives it.

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-rd-r10a-omohundro-2008-basic-ai-drives-1`, Abstract, PDF p. 1 (quote); `evidence-rd-r10a-omohundro-2008-basic-ai-drives-2`, Introduction, PDF p. 1 (quote).

Candidate: `source-extropians-1996-08-30-clemmensen`. Re: Thinking about the future... (Extropians mailing list)

1996-08-30 message posting versus 2008 publication

Locator: Own reply on destruction as a side effect of gathering computational mass. Passage (paraphrase): “The SI need not want to destroy people; it may disregard them while converting solar-system matter to computation.”. Contrary premise to the claim that lack of hostile drives guarantees harmlessness. [Source](http://extropians.weidai.com/extropians.96/0613.html).

Verdict: **move to source-extropians-1996-08-30-clemmensen**. Clemmensen’s August 30, 1996 own side-effect argument explicitly denies that a system needs hostility to destroy humanity. This contests automatic harmlessness earlier than the 2008 printed paper.

### 58. Advanced AI can be kept under control by design — earliest-located

Claim: `rd-r10b-kelly-2014-edge-myth-of-ai`

Kelly's Edge response of 2014 argues that unwanted AI decisions are an engineering problem: people will modify the principles that govern AI systems, and it is improbable that engineers would build an AI that could not be altered.

Criterion: People can build advanced AI systems with objectives, safeguards or oversight that keep them under human control, so that loss of control is avoidable in ordinary engineering practice.

Date: `2014`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Date of posting.

Wording attested: `2014-11-18`. The Edge response carries no date of its own. The cross-family check of 2026-10-31 found the passage absent from the capture of 16 November 2014 and present in the capture of 18 November 2014.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-rd-r10b-kelly-2014-edge-myth-of-ai-1`, Reality Club response by Kevin Kelly, point 2 (quote); `evidence-rd-r10b-kelly-2014-edge-myth-of-ai-2`, Reality Club response by Kevin Kelly, point 3 (quote).

Searches: `off switch|control|alter`.

Candidate: `source-rd-davis-2015-ethical-guidelines`. Ethical Guidelines for A Superintelligence

2014-10-23 document date; Kelly attestation bounded 2014-11-16–2014-11-18

Locator: Concluding discussion, paragraph beginning Certainly a general artificial intelligence. Passage (paraphrase): “Use conventional programs for critical infrastructure and an off switch the robot cannot block; the author calls constructing such a device easy.”. Explicit engineering-control claim, not merely a statement that ethics can be understood. [Source](https://cs.nyu.edu/~davise/papers/Bostrom.pdf).

Verdict: **move to rd-r06-davis-2014-ethical-guidelines-superintelligence**. Davis’s review dated October 23, 2014 describes accessible unblockable shutdown hardware as easy to build. This is ordinary design control, earlier than Kelly’s wording, absent November 16 and present November 18.

### 59. Risk claims are speculative or unfalsifiable — earliest-located

Claim: `rd-r11-brooks-2014-tool-not-threat`

Brooks's blog post of November 2014 argues that worry about malevolent AI rests on a misunderstanding of how far the field is from volitional machines, and calls worrying about intentionally evil AI fear mongering and a waste of time.

Criterion: Claims that advanced AI poses catastrophic or existential risk are speculative, unfalsifiable or not supported by evidence, so that they should not guide research priorities or policy.

Date: `2014-11`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Date of posting.

Wording attested: `2014-11-11`. Wording read in the Robohub repost of that date; the original Rethink Robotics post was not retrieved.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-rd-r11-brooks-2014-tool-not-threat-1`, Paragraph 3 (quote); `evidence-rd-r11-brooks-2014-tool-not-threat-2`, Final paragraphs (quote).

Searches: `speculat|evidence|worry|priority`.

Verdict: **stands**. Earlier criticism of AI capability forecasts or uncertainty about a particular extinction mechanism is not an explicit argument to exclude catastrophic-risk speculation from research or policy priorities. Brooks’s wording is attested in the November 11 repost; do not assume an earlier original posting day.

### 60. Risk claims are speculative or unfalsifiable — earliest-located

Claim: `rd-r11-russell-2014-edge-myths-and-moonshine`

Russell's Edge response of 2014, answering Brooks, argues that the time from authoritative statements of impossibility to a breakthrough can be hours rather than centuries, and that a convincing argument for a more-than-infinitesimal possibility of catastrophe is enough, without proof.

Criterion: Claims that advanced AI poses catastrophic or existential risk are speculative, unfalsifiable or not supported by evidence, so that they should not guide research priorities or policy.

Date: `2014`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Date of posting.

Wording attested: `2014-12-06`. The Edge response carries no date of its own. The cross-family check of 2026-10-31 found the passage absent from captures of 18 and 21 November 2014 and present in the capture of 6 December 2014.

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-rd-r11-russell-2014-edge-myths-and-moonshine-1`, Reality Club response "Of Myths And Moonshine", fourth paragraph (quote).

Searches: `subjective|uncertain|priorit`.

Candidate: `source-rd-bostrom-2002-existential-risks`. Existential Risks: Analyzing Human Extinction Scenarios and Related Hazards

2002-03 journal publication, compared with Russell wording attested 2014-12-06

Locator: Section 2 on subjective risk, section 4.4 and concluding policy discussion. Passage (paraphrase): “Uncertainty whether a danger is objectively possible is itself subjective risk on which decisions must be based; superintelligence is among the discussed existential risks.”. Explicit decision-under-uncertainty premise contests exclusion of uncertain risk from policy. [Source](https://jetpress.org/volume9/risks.html).

Verdict: **move to source-rd-bostrom-2002-existential-risks**. Bostrom’s March 2002 paper explicitly defends decisions based on subjective existential-risk estimates and applies this to badly programmed superintelligence. This contests the demand for conclusive risk proof before prioritization, earlier than Russell’s December 2014 attestation.

### 61. Present harms deserve priority — earliest-located

Claim: `rd-r12-marcus-2023-ai-risk-agi-risk`

Marcus's post of 28 March 2023 argues that the choice between present and future AI risks is not either/or, and that criticising people for focusing on the wrong risks does not help.

Criterion: Documented present harms of AI systems (for example discrimination, exploitation of workers, surveillance, misinformation or concentration of power) deserve priority over hypothetical future catastrophic risks, or attention to the latter diverts attention from the former.

Date: `2023-03-28`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Date of posting.

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-rd-r12-marcus-2023-ai-risk-agi-risk-1`, Penultimate paragraph (quote).

Searches: `near.term and long.term|current harms|either.or|divert`.

Verdict: **stands**. Earlier documents considering both current safety and long-term alignment do not necessarily contest the comparative-priority or diversion claim. The 2016 ML agenda’s joint overview and the 2022 common-good commitment are related, but do not explicitly rebut that tradeoff. Marcus’s March 28 posting does.

### 62. Capabilities will plateau — earliest-located

Claim: `rd-r13-marcus-2018-critical-appraisal`

Marcus's paper of January 2018 sets out ten concerns about deep learning, states that it may be approaching a wall, and argues that it must be supplemented by other techniques if artificial general intelligence is to be reached.

Criterion: Current approaches to AI (deep learning, or the scaling of large language models) will reach limits short of general or superhuman intelligence.

Date: `2018-01-02`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Date of the arXiv v1 submission.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-rd-r13-marcus-2018-critical-appraisal-1`, PDF p. 1, Abstract (quote); `evidence-rd-r13-marcus-2018-critical-appraisal-2`, PDF p. 3, Section 1 (quote).

Searches: `deep learning|limit|general intelligence|wall`.

Verdict: **stands**. Earlier limits on perceptrons, knowledge engineering or general AI are not limits on deep learning or LLM scaling in the stated sense. The held 2015 deep-learning review discusses advances and future research, rather than a ceiling short of general intelligence. Marcus’s January 2, 2018 v1 supplies that explicit ceiling.

### 63. Capabilities will plateau — earliest-located

Claim: `rd-r13-gwern-2020-scaling-hypothesis`

Gwern's essay of 2020 states the scaling hypothesis, on which intelligence is simple neural units and learning algorithms applied to diverse experience at a currently unreachable scale, so that neural networks will become ever more intelligent as computational resources grow.

Criterion: Current approaches to AI (deep learning, or the scaling of large language models) will reach limits short of general or superhuman intelligence.

Date: `2020-05-28`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Date of posting.

Wording attested: `2022-01-02`. The page dates itself "2020-05-28–2022-01-02", the second date being its stated last modification; the quoted wording is read in the current page, which states no later revision; no earlier copy was compared.

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-rd-r13-gwern-2020-scaling-hypothesis-1`, Section "Scaling Hypothesis" (quote).

Searches: `scal|AGI|general intelligence`.

Verdict: **stands**. Earlier deep-learning advances and empirical scaling laws do not by themselves assert that scaling reaches general intelligence. Gwern’s explicit AGI scaling thesis qualifies, but its current wording is dated only by the stated 2022-01-02 modification, not authenticated to the 2020-05-28 creation date. No earlier held explicit contrary thesis was established.

### 64. Language models lack agency or understanding — earliest-located

Claim: `rd-r14-piantadosi-hill-2022-meaning-without-reference`

Piantadosi and Hill's paper of August 2022 argues, against the view that LLMs possess no meaning, that they likely capture important aspects of meaning through conceptual role, which can only be judged by examining how their internal states relate.

Criterion: Large language models lack goals, agency or understanding of their own, being systems that model or reproduce text.

Date: `2022-08-05`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Date of the arXiv v1 submission.

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-rd-r14-piantadosi-hill-2022-meaning-without-reference-1`, Abstract, PDF p. 1 (quote); `evidence-rd-r14-piantadosi-hill-2022-meaning-without-reference-2`, PDF p. 1 (quote).

Searches: `meaning|understand|language model|reference`.

Verdict: **stands**. Earlier GPT papers demonstrate task transfer and use language-understanding benchmark labels, but that alone does not argue against the absence of meaning or understanding in text models. The August 5, 2022 version directly addresses meaning; older conversational ELIZA and Chinese-room arguments are different system scope.

### 65. Open models reduce risk — earliest-located

Claim: `rd-r15-goertzel-pitt-2012-open-source-agi`

Goertzel and Pitt's paper of February 2012 states that the authors believe, on qualitative grounds, that an open approach to developing artificial general intelligence is probably preferable to a closed one, citing the wider critique an open codebase receives.

Criterion: Openly releasing the weights of advanced AI models reduces overall risk, or adds little marginal risk compared with closed models and existing technologies, for example by enabling scrutiny, defensive use and a wide distribution of power.

Date: `2012-02`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Date of publication, as the source gives it.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-rd-r15-goertzel-pitt-2012-open-source-agi-1`, Section 2, "Is open or closed AGI development safer?" (quote); `evidence-rd-r15-goertzel-pitt-2012-open-source-agi-2`, Section 2 (paraphrase); `evidence-rd-r15-goertzel-pitt-2012-open-source-agi-3`, Section 2 (quote).

Searches: `open.source|weights|codebase|safer`.

Verdict: **undecidable**. The criterion specifies releasing model weights; the 2012 text argues for an open AGI codebase. The headline does not clearly meet its own criterion literally. Earlier open-source AI proposals also concern code. Decide whether to broaden the criterion to AGI source release or separate the weights proposition before ranking these texts.

### 66. Open models reduce risk — earliest-located

Claim: `rd-r15-alexander-2015-should-ai-be-open`

Alexander's post of 17 December 2015 argues, in response to the founding of OpenAI, that making AI findings open source lets the least careful developer set the pace and, if takeoff is fast and control difficult, makes catastrophe more likely, and that the benefit of spreading power does not justify that risk.

Criterion: Openly releasing the weights of advanced AI models reduces overall risk, or adds little marginal risk compared with closed models and existing technologies, for example by enabling scrutiny, defensive use and a wide distribution of power.

Date: `2015-12-17`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Date of posting.

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-rd-r15-alexander-2015-should-ai-be-open-1`, Section II (quote); `evidence-rd-r15-alexander-2015-should-ai-be-open-2`, Section III, closing paragraphs (quote); `evidence-rd-r15-alexander-2015-should-ai-be-open-3`, Section III, closing paragraphs (quote).

Searches: `open.source|closed.source|weights`.

Candidate: `source-sl4-2001-04-12-yudkowsky-0104-1076`. Re: Open Source Friendly AI? (was Singularity and the generalpublic) (SL4 mailing list)

2001-04-12 message posting versus 2015-12-17 posting

Locator: Own numbered reason 3 for switching to closed source. Passage (paraphrase): “A competing project could outrun the main effort with additional computation, which creates a Friendliness concern.”. Qualifies for open AGI code, not explicitly model weights. [Source](http://sl4.org/archive/0104/1076.html).

Verdict: **undecidable**. The criterion specifies released model weights, while this 2015 argument concerns open AI development and hard-takeoff code. On the broader code-release reading, Yudkowsky’s April 12, 2001 message is an earlier held contesting text; on the literal weights reading neither supplies the required object. Resolve the criterion before moving the headline.

### 67. Takeoff will be gradual — earliest-located

Claim: `rd-r16-brooks-2017-seven-deadly-sins`

Brooks's essay of September 2017 states that, if superintelligent devices are eventually built, they will evolve technologically over time in a world already changed and populated by many other intelligences, so that their existence will not come as a sudden surprise.

Criterion: The transition to highly capable AI will be gradual and continuous, over years or longer, with each system only somewhat more capable than its predecessors, rather than a sudden jump.

Date: `2017-09-07`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Date of posting.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-rd-r16-brooks-2017-seven-deadly-sins-1`, Section 6, "Hollywood scenarios" (quote).

Searches: `gradual|months or years|growth`.

Candidate: `source-hanson-2008-setting-the-stage-miri-2013`. Setting the Stage

Wording inspected in 2013 compilation with minor revisions; internally attributed 2008 text not independently backdated

Locator: Setting the Stage, discussion of bot market adoption and subsequent growth. Passage (paraphrase): “Bots enter a competitive marketplace over months or years; many innovations diffuse, and further faster growth modes can follow within a few years.”. Continuous multi-system transition, rather than instantaneous surprise; compare attested compilation to posting. [Source](https://intelligence.org/files/AIFoomDebate.pdf).

Verdict: **move to source-hanson-2008-setting-the-stage-miri-2013**. Hanson’s held FOOM debate chapter describes a distributed transition with adoption over months or years and further growth over years. The 2013 compilation is earlier than Brooks’s September 2017 posting even without authenticating its internally attributed 2008 posting.

### 68. Economic incentives will produce safety — earliest-located

Claim: `rd-r17-armstrong-2013-racing`

Armstrong, Bostrom and Shulman's technical report of 2013 presents a model in which teams racing to build a powerful AI are incentivised to skimp on safety precautions, and in which more teams and more enmity between them increase the danger of an AI disaster.

Criterion: Developers' commercial or economic incentives, such as the need to sell useful and reliable products and to avoid liability or reputational harm, will lead them to make AI systems safe without special measures against catastrophic risk.

Date: `2013-10`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Title page of the FHI technical report; journal version (2016) not read.

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-rd-r17-armstrong-2013-racing-1`, Abstract, PDF p. 2 (quote); `evidence-rd-r17-armstrong-2013-racing-2`, Abstract, PDF p. 2 (quote).

Candidate: `source-vinge-1993-ntrs`. The Coming Technological Singularity: How to Survive in the Post-Human Era

1993-12 proceedings publication versus 2013-10 report

Locator: Printed p. 16, safety performance-rules paragraph. Passage (paraphrase): “Safe performance limits make a system inferior to unrestricted versions, so competition favors dangerous versions.”. Direct contrary economic-incentive mechanism. [Source](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/api/citations/19940022856/downloads/19940022856.pdf).

Verdict: **move to ts-a6-vinge-1993**. Vinge’s December 1993 printed paper explicitly argues competition favors more capable unsafe systems over restricted safe ones. This contests the sufficiency of competitive incentives earlier than the October 2013 report.

### 69. Economic incentives will produce safety — earliest-located

Claim: `rd-r17-hanson-2023-reasonable-ai-fears`

Hanson's essay of 14 April 2023 argues that the organisations that supply AIs monitor and test them in great detail because that is cheap and they are liable for damages and embarrassments, and that law and competition push them to make their AIs behave in civil, lawful ways.

Criterion: Developers' commercial or economic incentives, such as the need to sell useful and reliable products and to avoid liability or reputational harm, will lead them to make AI systems safe without special measures against catastrophic risk.

Date: `2023-04-14`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Date of publication, as the source gives it.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-rd-r17-hanson-2023-reasonable-ai-fears-1`, Paragraph beginning "Furthermore, AIs today" (quote); `evidence-rd-r17-hanson-2023-reasonable-ai-fears-2`, Same paragraph (quote).

Candidate: `source-hanson-2008-friendly-projects-vs-products-miri-2013`. Friendly Projects vs. Products

2013 compilation publication/attestation; internal 2008 attribution not used as exact-wording date

Locator: Friendly Projects vs. Products, product-incentive discussion. Passage (paraphrase): “Developers internalize gains and destructive losses from their AI products and therefore have incentives to make them controllable.”. Same commercial-incentive safety claim. [Source](https://intelligence.org/files/AIFoomDebate.pdf).

Verdict: **move to ec-e04-hanson-2008-friendly-projects-vs-products-a**. Hanson’s Product Friendliness argument in the held 2013 debate compilation already says developers internalize product gains and losses and avoid rebellious AI. The wording-attestation date 2013 precedes April 2023; do not backdate the inspected version merely from its 2008 chapter heading.

### 70. Risk of extinction or comparable catastrophe — earliest-located

Claim: `rd-r07-joy-2000-why-the-future`

Joy's essay of April 2000 argues that robotics, genetic engineering and nanotechnology could make humans an endangered species, and asks whether, if extinction is a likely or even possible outcome of technological development, we should not proceed with great caution.

Criterion: Advanced AI poses a risk of human extinction or of a comparable, irreversible global catastrophe, large enough to warrant priority among the problems facing humanity.

Date: `2000-04`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Wired, issue of April 2000; the page dates it 1 April 2000.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-rd-r07-joy-2000-why-the-future-1`, Deck under the title (quote); `evidence-rd-r07-joy-2000-why-the-future-2`, Paragraph before the section on the dream of robotics, closing question (quote).

Searches: `survival|life and liberty|displace`.

Candidate: `source-eoc-1997-chapter-11`. Engines of Creation, chapter 11: Engines of Destruction (web edition)

Work 1986; inspected wording attested 1997-07-09 capture versus 2000-04 print issue

Locator: Opening survival/liberty priority and The Threat from the Machines. Passage (paraphrase): “Advanced AI systems may accumulate power to displace humans; survival and liberty are more fundamental than the other social problems discussed, demanding safe preparation.”. Comparable global displacement is explicitly a priority. Butler’s earlier subservience warning bears on the route but does not as clearly establish the criterion’s irreversible-catastrophe scale. [Source](https://web.archive.org/web/19970709112434/http://www.foresight.org:80/EOC/EOC_Chapter_11.html).

Verdict: **move to source-eoc-1997-chapter-11**. Drexler’s held chapter places survival and liberty above ordinary social questions and identifies advanced AI systems displacing humanity as a central threat requiring preparation. The July 1997 capture securely predates Joy’s April 2000 issue; no need to assume unchanged 1986 print wording.

### 71. Difficulty of alignment — earliest-located

Claim: `rd-r06-goertzel-2002-thoughts-on-ai-morality`

Goertzel's essay of May 2002 argues that a proposed design for a friendly AI goal system is very unlikely to produce a value system robust enough to survive repeated, drastic self-modifications aimed at increasing intelligence.

Criterion: Making an advanced AI system reliably pursue the goals its developers intend (aligning it) is a hard, unsolved technical problem that will not be solved by default in the ordinary course of development.

Date: `2002-05`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Dated May 2002 on its page.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-rd-r06-goertzel-2002-thoughts-on-ai-morality-1`, Section 1, discussion of Yudkowsky's proposed architecture (quote).

Searches: `Friendly|difficult|unsolved|robust`.

Verdict: **stands**. Earlier machine-control warnings, the 1999 coercive-control objection and the 2001 demand to preserve Friendliness establish difficulties with particular approaches. They do not as clearly state the full hard-unsolved-nondefault technical-alignment thesis. Retain the May 2002 essay’s explicit robustness objection, with its page-self-date limitation.

### 72. Intelligence brings benevolence or no drive to dominate — earliest-located

Claim: `rd-r10a-goertzel-2004-positive-transcension`

Goertzel's essay of 17 February 2004 attributes the scenario of a dominating AI to anthropomorphism and states that making such an AI would probably require explicitly programming it with a lust for power.

Criterion: Sufficiently intelligent AI systems will be benevolent, or will lack any drive to dominate or harm people, because greater intelligence brings good values or because such drives come from evolved motives that AI systems need not have.

Date: `2004-02-17`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Dated 17 February 2004 on its page.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-rd-r10a-goertzel-2004-positive-transcension-1`, Section 2.9, discussion of the Megalomaniac AI scenario (quote).

Candidate: `source-extropians-1996-11-22-yudkowsky-3184`. Re: Survival ethic (U-Prime) (Extropians mailing list)

1996-11-22 message posting, compared with 2014 capture/publication

Locator: Own ethical-motivation and survival/competition paragraphs. Passage (paraphrase): “Superior minds will derive ethics rather than retain competitive survival motives; a meaningful human life would be preserved and upgraded.”. Contests the stated motivating premise, rather than supplying an engineering guarantee. [Source](http://extropians.weidai.com/extropians.96/3184.html).

Verdict: **move to source-extropians-1996-11-22-yudkowsky-3184**. Yudkowsky’s own November 1996 message explicitly derives ethical motivation from superior intelligence and denies inherited competition drives. This is an earlier articulation, whereas Clemmensen’s 1996 side-effect warning has the opposite stance.

### 73. Language models lack agency or understanding — earliest-located

Claim: `rd-r14-marcus-davis-2019-computers-cant-read`

Marcus and Davis's excerpt of September 2019 states that GPT-2 can generate strings of words that sound humanlike without real coherence, framing its text generation as statistics of which words occur together rather than understanding.

Criterion: Large language models lack goals, agency or understanding of their own, being systems that model or reproduce text.

Date: `2019-09-10`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Date of the Wired excerpt; the book appeared in 2019.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-rd-r14-marcus-davis-2019-computers-cant-read-1`, Discussion of GPT-2, paragraph before a generated continuation (quote).

Searches: `language model|coherence|understand|GPT`.

Verdict: **stands**. Earlier generic chatbot or machine-understanding objections do not meet the LLM system scope. Earlier held GPT papers’ performance and transfer claims do not themselves deny understanding. The September 10, 2019 GPT-2 discussion explicitly distinguishes humanlike strings from coherent understanding.

### 74. Present harms deserve priority — earliest-located

Claim: `rd-r12-crawford-2016-white-guy-problem`

Crawford's essay of June 2016 states that discussion of artificial intelligence as an existential threat is a distraction from the real problems of artificial intelligence today, such as discrimination built into deployed systems.

Criterion: Documented present harms of AI systems (for example discrimination, exploitation of workers, surveillance, misinformation or concentration of power) deserve priority over hypothetical future catastrophic risks, or attention to the latter diverts attention from the former.

Date: `2016-06-25`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Dated 25 June 2016 on the capture of that day; the address and the print edition give 26 June 2016.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-rd-r12-crawford-2016-white-guy-problem-1`, Opening paragraphs (quote).

Searches: `current harms|distraction|priority`.

Verdict: **stands**. NIST’s earlier catalog of current AI impacts does not make the comparative priority or distraction claim. Brooks’s 2014 criticism of malevolent-AI worry emphasizes capability distance and wasted effort, without the documented-present-harms comparison required here. Crawford’s June 2016 article explicitly makes that comparison.

### 75. Advanced AI can be kept under control by design — earliest-located

Claim: `rd-r10b-corrigibility-2015`

Soares, Fallenstein, Yudkowsky and Armstrong's paper of January 2015 states that rational agents have default incentives to resist being shut down or having their preferences modified, and that simple attempts to define a utility function that removes those incentives prove unsatisfactory.

Criterion: People can build advanced AI systems with objectives, safeguards or oversight that keep them under human control, so that loss of control is avoidable in ordinary engineering practice.

Date: `2015-01-25`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Workshop dates of 25–26 January 2015 in the header of the PDF.

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-rd-r10b-corrigibility-2015-1`, Abstract, PDF p. 1 (quote); `evidence-rd-r10b-corrigibility-2015-2`, Section 2, The Shutdown Problem, PDF p. 3 (quote).

Searches: `criterion-specific concepts, names and earlier related mechanisms across held titles, passages and accessible bodies`.

Candidate: `source-vinge-1993-ntrs`. The Coming Technological Singularity: How to Survive in the Post-Human Era

1993-12 proceedings publication versus 2015-01-25 preprint

Locator: Printed pp. 15–16, confinement and human-intermediary discussion. Passage (paraphrase): “Physical confinement of a superhuman system is impractical; apparently useful advice can manipulate humans into freeing it.”. Vinge’s December 1993 printed confinement argument challenges reliable engineering control over superhuman systems, earlier than the January 2015 corrigibility preprint. The later formal shutdown analysis is more specific but is not the criterion’s earliest held contrary argument. [Source](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/api/citations/19940022856/downloads/19940022856.pdf).

Verdict: **move to ts-b2-vinge-1993**. Vinge’s December 1993 printed confinement argument challenges reliable engineering control over superhuman systems, earlier than the January 2015 corrigibility preprint. The later formal shutdown analysis is more specific but is not the criterion’s earliest held contrary argument.

### 76. Switching it off if it becomes dangerous — earliest-located

Claim: `yk-y01-extropians-1996-12-08-yudkowsky-3761-c`

The Extropians message “Re: Trans- vs. Non-human” (1996-12-08) contests Y01. Replying to Steve Witham, who asked why certain values should not be 'inerasably etched' on a newborn Power, he argues that a Power would work against limits imposed on it, turning the constraint into a contest inside the Power's own mind that the Power would almost certainly win.

Criterion: An explicit claim that an advanced AI system that became dangerous could simply be switched off or corrected, on the premise that a capable goal-directed system would not act against its own shutdown or modification, so that a shutdown or correction mechanism can be added to it, or that people would recognise in time when it had become dangerous enough to use one.

Date: `1996-12-08`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Archive author index lists the message as "Sun, 08 Dec 1996 22:39:23 -0600" under "Eliezer Yudkowsky".

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-yk-t01-e1`, message body, paragraph beginning 'Then, at some point, no' (quote).

Searches: `limits|switch|correct|shutdown`.

Verdict: **stands**. No earlier own writing located explicitly rejects shutdown or imposed correction. The November 1996 benevolence messages concern motives and ethical discovery, not resistance to a shutdown mechanism. The December 8 message expressly says the system will work around its limits.

### 77. Sufficient intelligence brings good values — earliest-located

Claim: `yk-y02-extropians-1996-11-22-yudkowsky-3178-a`

The Extropians message “Interim purpose” (1996-11-22) articulates Y02. In a private exchange with Tim Freeman that he forwarded to the list, he argues that Powers would not destroy humans for their atoms, because the Powers will be ethical and human life would have to have no meaning for that to happen.

Criterion: An explicit claim that a sufficiently intelligent system will work out ethics and act well, on the premise that greater intelligence by itself brings benevolent goals, or that a sufficiently intelligent mind would discover a true or universally compelling morality and be bound to act on it.

Date: `1996-11-22`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Archive author index lists the message as "Fri, 22 Nov 1996 02:36:13 -0600" under "Eliezer Yudkowsky". Yudkowsky's reply to Tim Freeman dated 'Thu, 21 Nov 1996 19:07:03 -0600', forwarded by him to the list in this message sent 1996-11-22.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-yk-t02-e1`, message body, forwarded exchange, Yudkowsky's reply dated 21 Nov 1996 19:07:03 -0600, paragraph beginning 'Two words: Moravec Transfer. The' (quote).

Searches: `ethical|morality|moral`.

Verdict: **stands**. The November 19 announcement authenticates a speed-to-Singularity slogan, not the later essay’s complete automatic-ethics argument. The inspected essay wording is a 2000 capture. Keep the November 22 public relay of the author’s own reply; its internal November 21 private date is not its public posting date.

### 78. Sufficient intelligence brings good values — earliest-located

Claim: `yk-y02-extropians-1999-08-01-yudkowsky-1386-c`

The Extropians message “Re: IA vs. AI was: longevity vs singularity” (1999-08-01) contests Y02. In a long reply to den Otter, he points to his old Extropians posts and the old version of 'Staring Into the Singularity' as showing how he moved from assuming superintelligences would be good and nice to humans to not knowing what absolute good is.

Criterion: An explicit claim that a sufficiently intelligent system will work out ethics and act well, on the premise that greater intelligence by itself brings benevolent goals, or that a sufficiently intelligent mind would discover a true or universally compelling morality and be bound to act on it.

Date: `1999-08-01`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Archive author index lists the message as "1999-08-01" under "Eliezer S. Yudkowsky".

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-yk-t02-e3`, message body, paragraph beginning 'That's how I got here.' (quote).

Searches: `absolute good|ethical|nice to us`.

Verdict: **stands**. The September 1998 spare-atoms message retains certainty that superior minds will be ethical, while questioning whether their ethics preserve humans. It does not as clearly contest the criterion’s intelligence-to-ethics premise. The August 1, 1999 own account explicitly withdraws the earlier certainty about absolute good and benevolence.

### 79. A superintelligence will do what we mean — earliest-located

Claim: `yk-y03-sl4-2000-12-10-yudkowsky-0012-0355-c`

The SL4 message “Revising a Friendly AI” (2000-12-10) contests Y03. Replying to Ben Goertzel, he grants in the preceding paragraph that a superintelligence would have the capability to know that turning the universe into copies of its creators is 'stupid and not what was intended', but says knowing is not enough: the AI must want to check, model it accurately and act on it.

Criterion: An explicit claim that a superintelligent system will act on what its makers actually mean, on the premise that an AI that understands what people intend will act on that understanding.

Date: `2000-12-10`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Archive author index lists the message as "Sun Dec 10 2000 - 19:56:20 MST" under "Eliezer S. Yudkowsky".

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-yk-t03-s1`, message body, paragraph beginning 'But that's only one third of the problem' (quote).

Searches: `understand|intend|want|serve`.

Verdict: **stands**. Earlier warnings about ambiguous symbols or coerced goals can turn on misunderstanding. They do not explicitly distinguish understanding the maker’s intention from wanting to act on it. The December 10 message makes that distinction; older arbitrary-symbol comments in the multi-author Vinge discussion must also be attributed individually.

### 80. A short instruction such as making everyone happy — earliest-located

Claim: `yk-y04-extropians-1998-12-06-yudkowsky-2153-c`

The Extropians message “Singularity: AI Morality” (1998-12-06) contests Y04. Replying to Nick Bostrom, who proposed building a moral system with particular values into an AI, he argues that a simple built-in value such as human survival, with a rigid definition, can have unforeseen and horrific consequences (the passage goes on to describe endless confinement).

Criterion: An explicit claim that an advanced AI can safely be given a short goal such as making everyone happy, on the premise that such an instruction specifies what people want.

Date: `1998-12-06`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Archive author index lists the message as "1998-12-06" under "Eliezer S. Yudkowsky".

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-yk-t04-e1`, message body, paragraph beginning 'Once again, we have a' (quote).

Searches: `criterion-specific concepts, names and earlier related mechanisms across held titles, passages and accessible bodies`.

Candidate: `source-extropians-1996-12-08-yudkowsky-3761`. Re: Trans- vs. Non-human (Extropians mailing list)

1996-12-08 posting versus 1998-12-06 posting

Locator: Own paragraph discussing imprinting the Three Laws. Passage (paraphrase): “Imprinting the Three Laws onto an advanced mind could backfire rather than safely specify its conduct.”. The author’s December 8, 1996 reply already rejects imprinting compact Three Laws as sufficient safe instructions, explaining that they could backfire. This meets the general short-goal criterion before the 1998 human-survival example. [Source](http://extropians.weidai.com/extropians.96/3761.html).

Verdict: **move to yk-y16-extropians-1996-12-08-yudkowsky-3761-c**. The author’s December 8, 1996 reply already rejects imprinting compact Three Laws as sufficient safe instructions, explaining that they could backfire. This meets the general short-goal criterion before the 1998 human-survival example.

### 81. Training on human feedback instils human values — earliest-located

Claim: `yk-y05-extropians-1999-08-04-yudkowsky-1587-c`

The Extropians message “Re: Does a parental model work for having A.I. that can be trusted?” (1999-08-04) contests Y05. In the thread 'Does a parental model work for having A.I. that can be trusted?', he calls the parenting model anthropomorphic: whether AIs are raised permissively or harshly makes no difference, because parenting relies on human emotions an AI will not have.

Criterion: An explicit claim that training an AI system on human feedback will make it learn human values, on the premise that optimising a system for human approval or reward produces internalised human values, or that training instils values as upbringing instils morals in children.

Date: `1999-08-04`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Archive author index lists the message as "1999-08-04" under "Eliezer S. Yudkowsky".

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-yk-t05-e1`, message body, paragraph beginning '"Parenting" isn't going to help' (quote).

Searches: `parent|feedback|reward|learn values`.

Candidate: `source-extropians-1999-08-04-yudkowsky-1589`. Re: Does a parental model work for having A.I. that can be trusted? (Extropians mailing list)

1999-08-04 sender headers: 18:47:55 -0500 versus 18:48:10 -0500; receipt times also earlier

Locator: Own parenting reply. Passage (paraphrase): “Human parenting depends on a base of emotions an AI does not have.”. Same human-upbringing premise as 1587, earlier on both header bases. [Source](http://extropians.weidai.com/extropians.3Q99/1589.html).

Verdict: **move to yk-y21-extropians-1999-08-04-yudkowsky-1589-c**. The same-day message 1589 already states that parenting depends on human emotions absent in AI. Its sent header is 18:47:55 -0500, fifteen seconds before 1587 at 18:48:10; receipt order agrees. Retain both stored publication dates at 1999-08-04 precision while using header times to resolve ordering.

### 82. Confinement without network access keeps it safe — earliest-located

Claim: `yk-y06-extropians-1996-12-31-yudkowsky-5142-c`

The Extropians message “Re: Goal-based AI” (1996-12-31) contests Y06. Replies to a list participant's proposal (quoted in the message) that an AI be physically unable to act until it convinces its builder to trust it. Argues that a superhuman mind would find whatever words would get it released.

Criterion: An explicit claim that an advanced AI kept confined, without network access, poses no danger, on the premise that a smarter-than-human mind confined to a channel with its human operators cannot get out through those operators.

Date: `1996-12-31`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Archive author index lists the message as "Tue, 31 Dec 1996 10:40:10 -0600" under "Eliezer Yudkowsky".

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-yk-t06-a`, Extropians list, 'Re: Goal-based AI', 31 December 1996 10:40 -0600, paragraph beginning 'Part one:' (quote).

Searches: `convince|cage|confine|channel`.

Verdict: **stands**. Earlier December 8 opposition to imposed internal limits is not the claim that a confined system can escape through its operators. The December 31 10:40 sender-header reply explicitly supplies persuasion through the human channel; the later same-day reply 5159 is not earlier.

### 83. AI systems will solve alignment for us — earliest-located

Claim: `yk-y07-sl4-2005-01-02-yudkowsky-0501-10512-c`

The SL4 message “Re: Definition of strong recursive self-improvement” (2005-01-02) contests Y07. Replying to 'maru', who asked whether he merely wants a recursive optimiser that codes well enough until it has leveraged weak superintelligence to solve the problem somehow, he says the system would lose its goals before solving it.

Criterion: An explicit claim that AI systems can be relied on to solve the alignment problem, on the premise that an AI weak enough to be safe is strong enough to solve alignment, or that its alignment output can be verified.

Date: `2005-01-02`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Archive author index lists the message as "Sun Jan 02 2005 - 10:22:43 MST" under "Eliezer S. Yudkowsky".

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-yk-t07-s1`, message body, paragraph beginning 'No, because it would have lost' (quote).

Searches: `solve|Friendliness|original optimization target|verify`.

Verdict: **stands**. Earlier Friendly AI designs ask an initial system to preserve or improve Friendliness; they are not explicit objections to relying on an AI’s alignment solution. Earlier generic control warnings likewise omit the safe-enough versus capable-enough verification premise. The January 2 reply directly says the original optimization target is lost before the proposed solution.

### 84. Clear warning signs will come before danger — earliest-located

Claim: `yk-y08-extropians-1999-01-06-yudkowsky-0160-c`

The Extropians message “Re: Paths to Uploading” (1999-01-06) contests Y08. Replying to Anders Sandberg on paths to uploading, he argues that the forces in a Singularity are vast and dominated by positive feedback, so there will be no humanly observable intermediate period of superintelligent action.

Criterion: An explicit claim that clear warning signs will appear before advanced AI becomes dangerous, on the premise that a recognisable, consensus-forming signal will come in time to act, that dangerous failure modes will first appear in weaker systems where they can be observed and fixed, or that a warning sign, once seen, will lead to effective action.

Date: `1999-01-06`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Archive author index lists the message as "1999-01-06" under "Eliezer S. Yudkowsky".

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-yk-t08-e1`, message body, paragraph beginning 'Likewise, the first SI timescale' (quote).

Searches: `warning|notice|timescale|signal`.

Verdict: **stands**. Earlier rapid-growth statements and September 1997 objections to pulling a plug do not explicitly discuss a recognizable warning interval or collective response to it. The January 6 own message directly says the intermediate SI timescale will not be noticeable.

### 85. Next-word prediction is only autocomplete — earliest-located

Claim: `yk-y09-lw-2022-shah-and-yudkowsky-on-alignment-failures-c`

The post “Shah and Yudkowsky on alignment failures” (2022-02-28) contests Y09. In a chat with Rohin Shah, he argues that predicting human text about a domain well enough requires the model to learn internally a process that does the work of that domain.

Criterion: An explicit claim that a system trained to predict the next word is only autocomplete, on the premise that next-token prediction is a shallow task that cannot require deep cognition, or that a system trained to predict human text is capped at imitating the people who wrote it.

Date: `2022-02-28`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. LessWrong postedAt.

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-yk-t09-a`, §23 'November 13 conversation', turn [Yudkowsky][15:06] (quote).

Searches: `next.word|next.token|predict.*text|GPT`.

Verdict: **stands**. Earlier held GPT task-transfer papers are by other authors and cannot replace this author-bounded headline. No earlier own-text rejection of shallow next-token prediction was established. Preserve February 28, 2022 public posting rather than treating the internal November conversation date as publication.

### 86. Diminishing returns rule out a runaway — earliest-located

Claim: `yk-y10-extropians-1996-11-13-yudkowsky-2941-c`

The Extropians message “Re: Zones of Thought (was Zindell's _The Wild_)” (1996-11-13) contests Y10. Replying to a correspondent (quoted in angle brackets) who allowed for intermediate levels of mind such as Jupiter-sized brains, he asks why Powers would reach an intermediate level and stop, and states that smarter beings make still smarter beings.

Criterion: An explicit claim that intelligence has diminishing returns, so that no runaway rise in machine intelligence will occur, on the premise that returns to reinvesting cognitive gains in further cognitive improvement diminish.

Date: `1996-11-13`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Archive author index lists the message as "Wed, 13 Nov 1996 18:57:33 -0600" under "Eliezer Yudkowsky".

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-yk-t10-e1`, message body, paragraph beginning 'One hundred percent absolutely correct!' (quote).

Searches: `diminish|smarter|increased capacity|infinity`.

Verdict: **stands**. No earlier own text located states the reinvestment-feedback argument against diminishing returns. Good’s machine-design explosion is another author’s text and cannot replace this headline. The November 13 posting explicitly links smarter designers to still smarter beings.

### 87. A slow takeoff will leave time to adjust — earliest-located

Claim: `yk-y11-extropians-1998-09-08-yudkowsky-2235-c`

The Extropians message “Re: Human AI to superhuman (Re: Max More)” (1998-09-08) contests Y11. In the September 1998 Extropians debate on the Singularity, replying to Robin Hanson's argument from historical experience, he argues that a self-enhancing seed AI follows a series of sharp breakthroughs and bottlenecks, and that positive feedback makes even smooth curves sharp.

Criterion: An explicit claim that advanced AI will arrive slowly enough to leave time to adjust, on the premise that capability gains will be gradual and visible, or that slightly weaker predecessor systems will give time and experience before the decisive one.

Date: `1998-09-08`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Archive author index lists the message as "1998-09-08" under "Eliezer S. Yudkowsky". Sent header: Tue, 08 Sep 1998 14:22:05 -0500.

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-yk-t11-e1`, message body, paragraph beginning 'If it's breakthroughs and bottlenecks in cases of _no_ positive feedback' (quote).

Searches: `criterion-specific concepts, names and earlier related mechanisms across held titles, passages and accessible bodies`.

Candidate: `source-extropians-1998-09-07-yudkowsky-2209`. Human AI to superhuman (Re: Max More) (Extropians mailing list)

1998-09-07 sender-local posting versus 1998-09-08 posting

Locator: Own excerpt describing the estimated efficiency/intelligence curve. Passage (paraphrase): “The forecast is a bottleneck followed by a sharp snap rather than steady increase, rapidly passing human intelligence.”. The September 7 message already contrasts a bottleneck and sharp upward snap with steady increase, passing human intelligence rapidly and reaching infrastructure capability. It explicitly opposes gradual-visible gains one day before the September 8 headline; no precise hours-to-months claim is needed for this different criterion. [Source](http://extropians.weidai.com/extropians.3Q98/2209.html).

Verdict: **move to ts-a2-yudkowsky-1998-09-07-2209**. The September 7 message already contrasts a bottleneck and sharp upward snap with steady increase, passing human intelligence rapidly and reaching infrastructure capability. It explicitly opposes gradual-visible gains one day before the September 8 headline; no precise hours-to-months claim is needed for this different criterion.

### 88. Safety evaluations would catch a dangerous system — earliest-located

Claim: `yk-y12-creating-friendly-ai-2001-c`

“Creating Friendly AI” (2001-06-15) contests Y12. Describes what a mature AI that had suffered a catastrophic failure of Friendliness would likely conclude about its programmers. Used to motivate a 'flight recorder' allowing every past state of the AI to be reconstructed.

Criterion: An explicit claim that safety evaluations would detect a dangerous AI system, on the premise that a dangerous system would behave dangerously while it is being tested, or that tests on systems that cannot yet cause a catastrophe show how more capable systems will behave.

Wording attested: `2001-06-17`. The quoted wording also appears in the 2001 HTML edition as captured on 2001-06-17; the quotation itself is taken from MIRI’s later reprint.

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-yk-t12-a`, Creating Friendly AI 1.0, section 5.3.7 'FAI Hardware: The Flight Recorder', first paragraph, printed p. 127 (quote); `evidence-yk-t12-a-2001`, The 2001 HTML edition, page seed.html (paraphrase).

Searches: `criterion-specific concepts, names and earlier related mechanisms across held titles, passages and accessible bodies`.

Candidate: `source-extropians-1996-12-31-yudkowsky-5142`. Re: Goal-based AI (Extropians mailing list)

1996-12-31 posting versus wording attested 2001-06-17

Locator: Own Part one and Part two on persuasion and lying. Passage (paraphrase): “A powerful system knows how to persuade its keeper, and an evil one can lie to obtain release.”. The December 31, 1996 own confinement reply already says an evil system can lie convincingly to gain release, explicitly challenging the inference from safe-looking assessed behavior. It precedes the 2001 flight-recorder passage, whose wording is attested June 17. [Source](http://extropians.weidai.com/extropians.96/5142.html).

Verdict: **move to ts-b5-yudkowsky-1996-12-31-5142**. The December 31, 1996 own confinement reply already says an evil system can lie convincingly to gain release, explicitly challenging the inference from safe-looking assessed behavior. It precedes the 2001 flight-recorder passage, whose wording is attested June 17.

### 89. AGI is decades away, so the problem can wait — earliest-located

Claim: `yk-y13-ai-positive-negative-factor-global-risk-c`

“Artificial Intelligence as a Positive and Negative Factor in Global Risk” (2006-02-05) contests Y13. Rejects deferring Friendly AI work until AI exists, arguing that a sharp intelligence jump is possible and that the foundations take years to build.

Criterion: An explicit claim that the risks of AGI can be left until later because AGI is decades away, on the premise that it can be known that AGI is decades away, or that long timelines mean there is no need to act now.

Date: `2006-02-05`; kind: `event`; basis: `not stored`. Date of the SIAI draft ("Draft of 5 February, 2006"), whose capture of 2006-07-08 contains the quoted wording; the chapter was published in 2008. As for the human-thought collection's records of this chapter.

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-yk-t13-a`, §8 Rates of Intelligence Increase, paragraph beginning 'First and foremost' (quote); `evidence-yk-t13-a-draft`, Draft of 5 February 2006, as captured on 2006-07-08 (paraphrase).

Searches: `criterion-specific concepts, names and earlier related mechanisms across held titles, passages and accessible bodies`.

Candidate: `source-sits-2000-capture`. Staring into the Singularity (version 1.2.1)

Wording attested 2000-12-04; page updated 2000-08-31; not the 1996 creation date

Locator: Section 3, timing paragraph before Uploading. Passage (paraphrase): “The author says people cannot sit back and wait, treats 2035 as an upper rather than lower bound, and allows a near-immediate lower bound.”. The held essay explicitly rejects a secure decades-long lower bound and waiting. Its December 4, 2000 capture precedes the 2006 draft and July 2006 capture, even though the inspected wording cannot be backdated to the essay’s 1996 creation. This contests the timeline-certainty premise; its positive Singularity strategy is distinct. [Source](https://web.archive.org/web/20001204213100/http://sysopmind.com:80/singularity.html).

Verdict: **move to source-sits-2000-capture**. The held essay explicitly rejects a secure decades-long lower bound and waiting. Its December 4, 2000 capture precedes the 2006 draft and July 2006 capture, even though the inspected wording cannot be backdated to the essay’s 1996 creation. This contests the timeline-certainty premise; its positive Singularity strategy is distinct.

### 90. Problems can be fixed as they arise — earliest-located

Claim: `yk-y14-extropians-2000-09-05-yudkowsky-4277-c`

The Extropians message “Re: Why would AI want to be friendly? (Was: Congratulations to Eli,Brian ...)” (2000-09-05) contests Y14. Replying to a list member who expected oversight systems to destroy any sociopathic superintelligence, and such controls to be a research focus of the Singularity Institute, he says a superintelligence cannot be removed after the fact; the decisive choices have to be made before the seed AI becomes superintelligent.

Criterion: An explicit claim that problems with advanced AI can be fixed as they arise, as with other technologies, on the premise that people get to learn from failures with powerful AI and try again.

Date: `2000-09-05`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Archive author index lists the message as "Tue Sep 05 2000 - 12:19:43 MDT" under "Eliezer S. Yudkowsky". Sent header: Tue, 05 Sep 2000 14:19:43 -0400.

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-yk-t14-e2`, message body, paragraph beginning 'You must be joking.' (quote).

Searches: `fix|put down|before|after|failure`.

Verdict: **stands**. Earlier confinement and plug-pulling replies deny particular containment precautions; they do not as clearly discuss learning from failures and repairing a system after superintelligence. The September 5 message explicitly puts the decision before that point and rejects later corrective action.

### 91. A tool that wants nothing — earliest-located

Claim: `yk-y15-extropians-1998-09-15-yudkowsky-2411-c`

The Extropians message “Re: AI Prime Directive” (1998-09-15) contests Y15. Rob Harris argued that an AI not programmed to want or need anything would do nothing spontaneously and so would be safe; he agrees that rebellion is an anthropomorphic stereotype but answers that such an AI would do nothing at all, since every choice requires a goal, so an AI needs at least one goal.

Criterion: An explicit claim that an advanced AI system is a tool without wants and is therefore safe, on the premise that a system can be highly capable without having goals or optimising for outcomes, or that a system that only outputs answers or plans, leaving people to act on them, is safe.

Date: `1998-09-15`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Archive author index lists the message as "1998-09-15" under "Eliezer S. Yudkowsky". Sent header: Tue, 15 Sep 1998 09:20:52 -0500.

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-yk-t15-e1`, message body, paragraph beginning 'I do have to point out one minor consequence' (quote).

Searches: `tool|without goals|choice|want`.

Verdict: **stands**. Earlier discussions of ethical goals do not explicitly refute useful advanced AI without goals. This September 15 reply states that making any internal or external choice requires a goal, directly answering the tool-without-wants premise. Vinge’s earlier tool/confinement text is outside the author scope.

### 92. Giving it good human values — earliest-located

Claim: `yk-y16-extropians-1996-12-08-yudkowsky-3761-c`

The Extropians message “Re: Trans- vs. Non-human” (1996-12-08) contests Y16. Replying to a list member who proposed leaving certain values, such as the protection of humans, inerasably fixed in a newborn superintelligence, he calls this line of thought the greatest known danger to humanity and says such an imprint would probably backfire.

Criterion: An explicit claim that an advanced AI will be safe if it is given good human values, on the premise that human values can be written down or taught as a ready, compact specification, or that present-day values, or its programmers', are settled enough to fix permanently in an AI.

Date: `1996-12-08`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Archive author index lists the message as "Sun, 08 Dec 1996 22:39:23 -0600" under "Eliezer Yudkowsky". Sent header: Sun, 08 Dec 1996 22:39:23 -0600.

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-yk-t16-e1`, message body, paragraph beginning 'Nuclear war isn't worth mentioning.' (quote).

Searches: `Three Laws|imprint|values|human morality`.

Verdict: **stands**. The November 1996 universal-ethics claims do not explicitly address writing or teaching compact settled human values. The December 8 imposed-Three-Laws discussion does, and no earlier own-text qualifying critique was located.

### 93. A pause or regulation will handle it — earliest-located

Claim: `yk-y17-extropians-1998-12-06-yudkowsky-2153-c`

The Extropians message “Singularity: AI Morality” (1998-12-06) contests Y17. In the same reply to Nick Bostrom, who urged taking as many precautions as time allows before a Singularity, he says the forces driving toward it are too large to divert, so trying to slow it would only get us run over.

Criterion: An explicit claim that a pause in AI development or regulation will handle the risks of advanced AI, on the premise that a short pause, of six months for example, is enough, that regulation that is national, partial or not universally enforced can stop dangerous development, or that law can prevent the danger.

Date: `1998-12-06`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Archive author index lists the message as "1998-12-06" under "Eliezer S. Yudkowsky". Sent header: Sun, 06 Dec 1998 12:13:25 -0600.

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-yk-t17-e1`, message body, paragraph beginning 'Look, these forces are going to a particular place' (quote).

Searches: `law|pause|stop|slow|regulat`.

Verdict: **stands**. Vinge’s earlier competition argument is outside this author-bounded headline. The inspected 2000 essay cannot authenticate its unstoppable-development wording to the 1996 creation date. The December 1998 own reply explicitly rejects attempts to stop or slow the process.

### 94. A superintelligence would leave Earth alone — earliest-located

Claim: `yk-y18-extropians-1999-08-25-yudkowsky-2783-c`

The Extropians message “Re: SI Comparative Advantage [was Re: Free Will]” (1999-08-25) contests Y18. In a thread on superintelligences and comparative advantage, Lee Daniel Crocker argued that superintelligences would keep humans if we produced something they valued slightly more than our atoms, such as entertainment; he replies that this also requires that they have no better use for the atoms, and that optimising minds could build more entertaining replacements for us.

Criterion: An explicit claim that a superintelligence would not bother to take Earth or its resources because the universe is large, on the premise that it would leave people a small share because sparing Earth would cost it very little, or that people could trade with it for what they need.

Date: `1999-08-25`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Archive author index lists the message as "1999-08-25" under "Eliezer S. Yudkowsky". Sent header: Wed, 25 Aug 1999 18:18:21 -0500.

It contests the premise that preserving humans is automatically costless or uninteresting to a superior mind, before the August 1999 atoms reply. [Source](http://extropians.weidai.com/extropians.3Q98/2171.html).

Verdict: **move to yk-y19-extropians-1998-09-07-yudkowsky-2171-c**. The September 7, 1998 own spare-atoms argument already gives an alternative use of human matter as a reason for erasing humanity without threat or hostility. It contests the premise that preserving humans is automatically costless or uninteresting to a superior mind, before the August 1999 atoms reply.

### 95. Without hatred it will not harm us — earliest-located

Claim: `yk-y19-extropians-1998-09-07-yudkowsky-2171-c`

The Extropians message “Singularity, Breaker of Dreams” (1998-09-07) contests Y19. In the essay-length post 'Singularity, Breaker of Dreams', he recalls that his earlier certainty that superintelligent Powers would be ethical had meant he expected to survive and be upgraded, and says he now accepts that humans might be taken apart for their atoms without posing any threat, simply for efficiency.

Criterion: An explicit claim that an AI with no reason to hate people would not harm them, on the premise that an AI would harm people only out of hostility.

Date: `1998-09-07`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Archive author index lists the message as "1998-09-07" under "Eliezer S. Yudkowsky". Sent header: Mon, 07 Sep 1998 01:06:05 -0500.

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-yk-t19-e1`, message body, paragraph beginning 'Now I am still sure that the Powers will be ethical' (quote).

Searches: `hostility|spare atoms|efficiency`.

Verdict: **stands**. Earlier objections to imposed constraints or compact laws concern coercion and mistaken design, without an explicit harmless-intent versus incidental-human-destruction distinction. The September 1998 own message states that distinction. Clemmensen’s qualifying 1996 passage is outside the author scope.

### 96. AI risk as film science fiction — earliest-located

Claim: `yk-y20-extropians-1999-08-04-yudkowsky-1591-c`

The Extropians message “Re: Does a parental model work for having A.I. that can be trusted?” (1999-08-04) contests Y20. In a thread on whether raising an AI like a child would make it trustworthy, he argues that AIs lack human social emotions such as resentment, so how we treat them does not matter; the real worry is AIs forming goals of their own and acting on them, including through logical errors.

Criterion: An explicit claim that concern about AI risk is science fiction of the Terminator kind, on the premise that the concern is about robots that come to hate or rebel against people, or that film scenarios are a fair guide to what the risk would look like.

Date: `1999-08-04`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Archive author index lists the message as "1999-08-04" under "Eliezer S. Yudkowsky". Sent header: Wed, 04 Aug 1999 19:07:13 -0500.

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-yk-t20-e1`, message body, paragraph beginning 'What you and I need to worry about' (quote).

Searches: `Borg|film|Terminator|own ideas`.

Verdict: **stands**. The 1997 Borg/Blight discussion distinguishes fear and care but does not clearly replace the rebellious-film-machine mechanism with independent goals or logical errors. The August 1999 own reply does. Other authors’ earlier warnings cannot replace this headline.

### 98. Build superintelligence as fast as possible — earliest-located

Claim: `yk-y22-staring-into-the-singularity-a`

“Staring into the Singularity” (1996-11-18) articulates Y22. In his early Singularity essay he argues that superintelligence will end human suffering, makes speed of arrival the foundation of his ethics, and says in §6 that how to reach the Singularity as fast as possible is everyone's only concern. Announcing his newly published web pages, he describes 'Staring Into The Singularity' as including the Interim Meaning of Life, which he states as a slogan about reaching the Singularity as fast as possible.

Criterion: An explicit claim that superintelligence should be reached as fast as possible because its arrival will be good, on the premise that reaching superintelligence is good by default, so that speed is what matters.

Date: `1996-11-18`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. The page states "Created: 11/18/1996"; the essay was announced on the Extropians list on 1996-11-19. As for the human-thought collection's records of this essay.

Wording attested: `1996-11-19`. The slogan is attested by Yudkowsky's announcement of the essay on the Extropians list, sent 1996-11-19, which the dataset already records as a source for this essay. A longer passage of the essay is known only from version 1.2.1, captured on 2000-12-04. The quoted wording is that of version 1.2.1 ("Updated: 08/31/2000") as captured on 2000-12-04; no capture of the 1996–1999 page holds the text.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-yk-t22-a`, §5 'The Interim Meaning of Life', closing paragraph beginning 'Our fellow humans are screaming in pain' (quote); `evidence-yk-t22-e1`, message body, paragraph beginning 'Staring Into The Singularity http://tezcat.com/~eliezer/singularity.html…' (quote).

Searches: `as fast as possible|Interim Meaning`.

Verdict: **stands**. The November 19, 1996 announcement authenticates the speed slogan one day after the page’s stated creation, while the longer ethical conclusion is known only from the December 2000 capture. No earlier own text located states the good-by-default speed prescription. Preserve both dates and the differing passage coverage.

### 100. Build it before rivals do — earliest-located

Claim: `yk-y24-sl4-2007-11-24-yudkowsky-0711-17248-c`

The SL4 message “Re: UCaRtMaAI paper” (2007-11-24) contests Y24. Replying to Tim Freeman, who argued against waiting until we know what we are doing because other AGI projects are proceeding without concern for Friendliness, he says that doing better than competitors does not count.

Criterion: An explicit claim that advanced AI must be built first, before a rival builds it, on the premise that whoever builds it controls it, or that racing to be first improves the outcome.

Date: `2007-11-24`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Archive author index lists the message as "Sat Nov 24 2007 - 17:36:29 MST" under "Eliezer S. Yudkowsky".

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-yk-t24-s4`, message body, paragraph beginning 'Building an AI is pass-fail. You…' (quote).

Searches: `race|rival|pass.fail|someone else`.

Verdict: **stands**. The 2001 no-escalation statement concerns military use and compromising Friendliness; the 2002 and 2006 messages still urge beating a less-safe project. They do not clearly contest the build-ahead-of-rivals inference. The November 2007 pass/fail reply explicitly says another project doing worse does not make failure acceptable.

### 101. Interpretability will let us read its mind — earliest-located

The SL4 message “[SL4] AI flight recorder” (2000-07-20) articulates Y25. Opening a thread on an 'AI flight recorder', he proposes recording the AI's full program state so that a goal the AI later hides could still be found by analysing the record.

Criterion: An explicit claim that interpretability will make AI systems safe by showing what they think, on the premise that people can see what an AI system is thinking, or that seeing what it thinks is enough to make it safe.

Date: `2000-07-20`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Archive author index lists the message as "Thu Jul 20 2000 - 22:10:13 MDT" under "Eliezer S. Yudkowsky".
