### 106. The developers know what they are doing — earliest-located

Claim: `yk-y28-sl4-2001-08-01-yudkowsky-0108-2031-c`

The SL4 message “Re: Floppy take-off” (2001-08-01) contests Y28. Replying to James Higgins on brute-force AI, he argues that with enough hardware the project that finishes first need not be the one that best understands intelligence or Friendliness.

Criterion: An explicit claim that the organisations building advanced AI can be relied on to handle its safety, on the premise that a credible safety plan exists, or that the most advanced projects are the most competent to handle safety.

Date: `2001-08-01`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Archive author index lists the message as "Wed Aug 01 2001 - 19:08:09 MDT" under "Eliezer S. Yudkowsky".

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-yk-t28-s1`, message body, paragraph beginning 'Brute-force AI is a way of…' (quote).

Searches: `earlier own and cross-collection texts by criterion; quotations and later revisions separated`.

Candidate: `source-sl4-2001-04-12-yudkowsky-0104-1076`. Re: Open Source Friendly AI? (was Singularity and the generalpublic) (SL4 mailing list)

2001-04-12 posting versus 2001-08-01 posting

Locator: Own numbered reason 3. Passage (paraphrase): “Extra computational power lets another project outrun the main one even when Friendliness is a concern.”. The April 12, 2001 own closed-source explanation already says a competitor could outrun the main project merely with additional computation, creating Friendliness risk. This explicitly separates the leading project from superior safety competence before the August message. [Source](http://sl4.org/archive/0104/1076.html).

Verdict: **move to source-sl4-2001-04-12-yudkowsky-0104-1076**. The April 12, 2001 own closed-source explanation already says a competitor could outrun the main project merely with additional computation, creating Friendliness risk. This explicitly separates the leading project from superior safety competence before the August message.

### 107. Many AIs will keep each other in check — earliest-located

Claim: `yk-y29-extropians-2000-03-16-yudkowsky-5105-c`

The Extropians message “Re: regarding the Eliezer vrs. Otter debate...” (2000-03-16) contests Y29. Replying to John Grigg's hope that 'an AI on our team' would neutralise a hostile AI armed with nanotechnology, he says there would be no teams because the first AI would enhance itself and take over.

Criterion: An explicit claim that many AI systems will keep one another in check, on the premise that several superintelligences would balance one another in people's favour.

Date: `2000-03-16`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Archive author index lists the message as "Thu Mar 16 2000 - 16:43:29 MST" under "Eliezer S. Yudkowsky".

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-yk-t29-e2`, message body, paragraph beginning 'No teams. Even if it's…' (quote).

Searches: `teams|balance|Sysop|multiple`.

Verdict: **stands**. Earlier forecasts of superior minds sharing ethics or one AI growing rapidly do not explicitly test the safety of multiple systems balancing each other. The March 16 no-teams reply directly rejects that balancing scenario and explains why the earlier entrant becomes Sysop.

### 108. It will probably turn out fine — earliest-located

Claim: `yk-y30-sl4-2000-11-13-yudkowsky-0011-0181-c`

The SL4 message “Re: Ben's "Extropian Creed"” (2000-11-13) contests Y30. Replying to Ben Goertzel, he rejects his own earlier belief that a good AI outcome would follow automatically, and in the next paragraph adds that cyber-transcendence cannot be assumed.

Criterion: An explicit claim that the development of advanced AI will probably turn out well, on the premise that the default outcome is good.

Date: `2000-11-13`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Archive author index lists the message as "Mon Nov 13 2000 - 22:45:54 MST" under "Eliezer S. Yudkowsky".

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-yk-t30-s1`, message body, paragraph beginning 'I used to have the extremely…' (quote).

Searches: `earlier own and cross-collection texts by criterion; quotations and later revisions separated`.

Candidate: `source-extropians-1999-08-01-yudkowsky-1386`. Re: IA vs. AI was: longevity vs singularity (Extropians mailing list)

1999-08-01 posting versus 2000-11-13 posting

Locator: Own changed-belief and survival-probability paragraphs. Passage (paraphrase): “Assured kindness and knowledge of absolute good are withdrawn; assessed survival chances vary widely, rather than following automatically.”. The August 1, 1999 own reply already withdraws assured benevolence and gives uncertain survival odds around a coin flip. It contests the default-good expectation earlier than the explicit November 2000 retrospective explanation; the earlier posting dates its own argument rather than the belief’s unrecorded onset. [Source](http://extropians.weidai.com/extropians.3Q99/1386.html).

Verdict: **move to yk-y02-extropians-1999-08-01-yudkowsky-1386-c**. The August 1, 1999 own reply already withdraws assured benevolence and gives uncertain survival odds around a coin flip. It contests the default-good expectation earlier than the explicit November 2000 retrospective explanation; the earlier posting dates its own argument rather than the belief’s unrecorded onset.

### 109. It will probably turn out fine — earliest-located

Claim: `yk-y30-extropians-1996-11-22-yudkowsky-3178-a`

The Extropians message “Interim purpose” (1996-11-22) articulates Y30. In the same forwarded email exchange with Tim Freeman, he asserts that superintelligences will be ethical and would not destroy humans unless human life had no meaning.

Criterion: An explicit claim that the development of advanced AI will probably turn out well, on the premise that the default outcome is good.

Date: `1996-11-22`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Archive author index lists the message as "Fri, 22 Nov 1996 02:36:13 -0600" under "Eliezer Yudkowsky". Date of his reply inside a private exchange he forwarded to the list on 1996-11-22.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-yk-t30-e1`, message body, his forwarded reply dated 21 Nov 1996 19:07:03 -0600, paragraph beginning 'Two words: Moravec Transfer. The…' (quote).

Searches: `good|ethical|survive|Singularity`.

Verdict: **stands**. The November 19 speed slogan is earlier public evidence for the desired speed, but does not by itself authenticate the automatic good-outcome premise in the longer essay. The November 22 own reply explicitly combines ethical certainty with survival. Preserve the internal private reply’s date separately from public relay.

### 110. Machines succeed humans as the leading intelligence — earliest-located

Claim: `ht-w01-degaris-1989-what-if-ai-succeeds`

De Garis's AI Magazine article of 1989 writes that in 20 years' time it will be commonplace to say that the twenty-first century will be dominated by the machine "if humanity so chooses—and maybe even if not". It also sets out a second scenario, which it calls "probably more popular in science fiction", in which the artilects take over and events might evolve too quickly for human beings to remain in control.

Criterion: Machine intelligence will or could replace or succeed human beings as the dominant or leading form of intelligence on Earth, or as the principal agent of further development, whether or not humans survive.

Date: `1989`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Date of publication, as the source gives it.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ht-w01-degaris-1989-what-if-ai-succeeds-1`, p. 20, 'As Machines Grow Smarter' (quote); `evidence-ht-w01-degaris-1989-what-if-ai-succeeds-2`, p. 21, 'Scenario 2' (quote).

Searches: `earlier own and cross-collection texts by criterion; quotations and later revisions separated`.

Candidate: `source-butler-1863-press`. Darwin among the Machines

1863-06-13 newspaper print date, original page reproduction; modern hosting/capture is not publication

Locator: Concluding paragraphs on mechanical life and machine supremacy. Passage (paraphrase): “Humans create their successors; machines will hold real supremacy over the world and its inhabitants.”. Butler’s held June 13, 1863 newspaper text explicitly predicts machines becoming the superior race with real supremacy over the world and humans. The printed page predates the 1989 magazine text; the criterion does not require modern electronic implementation. [Source](https://web.archive.org/web/20260826113039/https://paperspast.natlib.govt.nz/newspapers/CHP18630613.2.4).

Verdict: **move to ts-b6-butler-1863**. Butler’s held June 13, 1863 newspaper text explicitly predicts machines becoming the superior race with real supremacy over the world and humans. The printed page predates the 1989 magazine text; the criterion does not require modern electronic implementation.

### 111. Machines succeed humans as the leading intelligence — earliest-located

Claim: `ht-w01-lanier-2000-one-half-a-manifesto`

Lanier's essay of 2000 argues that software does not improve with Moore's Law in the way hardware does, and that treating technology as autonomous abdicates human responsibility, against the expectation that computers will become autonomous successors.

Criterion: Machine intelligence will or could replace or succeed human beings as the dominant or leading form of intelligence on Earth, or as the principal agent of further development, whether or not humans survive.

Date: `2000-11-10`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Edge dateline [11.10.00].

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ht-w01-lanier-2000-one-half-a-manifesto-1`, Heading "Belief #5" (quote); `evidence-ht-w01-lanier-2000-one-half-a-manifesto-2`, Section "Belief #5" (quote); `evidence-ht-w01-lanier-2000-one-half-a-manifesto-3`, Conclusion (quote).

Searches: `machine autonomy|succession|replace|reverse Moore`.

Verdict: **stands**. Earlier limitations on particular AI techniques and consciousness-transfer objections do not explicitly reject machine succession or the claim of autonomous technological dominance. Lanier’s November 2000 manifesto directly contests that autonomy premise. Preserve its publication date without deriving chronology from the current host.

### 112. Succession by machines would be acceptable or desirable — earliest-located

Claim: `ht-w02-minsky-1994-will-robots-inherit-the-earth`

Minsky's article of 1994 (revised web text), asking whether machines will replace us, states that he does not feel it makes much sense to think in terms of "us" and "them", and that he prefers Moravec's suggestion to think of future intelligent machines as "mind-children". It answers "Will robots inherit the earth?" with "Yes, but they will be our children."

Criterion: The replacement or succession of human beings by machine intelligence (W01) would be acceptable, desirable or worth pursuing, for example because machines would be humanity's descendants or because the growth of intelligence matters more than its human form.

Date: `1994-10`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Scientific American, October 1994.

Wording attested: `1997-01-02`. Revised web text, last modified 1997-01-02; not compared with the magazine pages.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ht-w02-minsky-1994-will-robots-inherit-the-earth-1`, Section "Limitations of Human Wisdom.", paragraph beginning "Does this mean that machines will replace us?" (quote); `evidence-ht-w02-minsky-1994-will-robots-inherit-the-earth-2`, Section "Limitations of Human Wisdom.", paragraph beginning "Does this mean that machines will replace us?" (quote); `evidence-ht-w02-minsky-1994-will-robots-inherit-the-earth-3`, Final paragraph, before "Further Reading" (quote).

Searches: `successor|descendants|help the process|mind.children`.

Candidate: `source-moravec-1989-takeover-file`. Human Culture—A Genetic Takeover Underway

Work attributed 1989, internal file July 1988; matching archive witness 2001-03-06. Compare with Minsky revised page last-modified 1997-01-02, not verified 1994 print.

Locator: Opening and closing succession argument. Passage (paraphrase): “The author describes humanity’s machine succession and recommends helping the process along.”. Qualifies in sense, but original printed wording and comparable earlier witness are not established. [Source](https://frc.ri.cmu.edu/~hpm/project.archive/general.articles/1988/takeover.ltx).

Verdict: **undecidable**. Earlier-attributed Moravec texts welcome machine succession, but the held Robot as Liberation file is a later author-hosted LaTeX text, not inspected December 1989 print, and Genetic Takeover is attested March 6, 2001. Minsky’s wording is itself a revised page self-dated January 2, 1997, not compared with October 1994 print. The earlier work dates do not decide exact-wording priority on a common basis.

### 113. Succession by machines would be acceptable or desirable — earliest-located

Claim: `ht-w02-joy-2000-why-the-future-doesnt-need-us`

Joy's article of 2000 quotes Moravec's Robot and states that Moravec's view is that robots will eventually succeed humans. On humans gradually replacing themselves with robotic technology by downloading, a prospect it connects with Hillis and Kurzweil, Joy writes that it seems to him far more likely that the robots would in no sense be human children and that on this path humanity may well be lost.

Criterion: The replacement or succession of human beings by machine intelligence (W01) would be acceptable, desirable or worth pursuing, for example because machines would be humanity's descendants or because the growth of intelligence matters more than its human form.

Date: `2000-04`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Wired 8.04, April 2000.

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ht-w02-joy-2000-why-the-future-doesnt-need-us-1`, Paragraph after the extended quotation from Robot (quote); `evidence-ht-w02-joy-2000-why-the-future-doesnt-need-us-2`, Paragraph beginning "But if we are downloaded into our technology" (after the paragraph beginning "A second dream of robotics") (quote).

Searches: `earlier own and cross-collection texts by criterion; quotations and later revisions separated`.

Candidate: `source-butler-1863-press`. Darwin among the Machines

1863-06-13 printed newspaper versus 2000-04 issue

Locator: Conclusion after the forecast of machine supremacy. Passage (paraphrase): “After predicting machine supremacy, the text urges destroying machinery to prevent the outcome.”. Butler’s 1863 newspaper text opposes accepting machine supremacy by calling for immediate destruction of machinery. It is an earlier held contesting response to the prospect, even though it also describes domesticated human survival rather than extinction. [Source](https://web.archive.org/web/20260826113039/https://paperspast.natlib.govt.nz/newspapers/CHP18630613.2.4).

Verdict: **move to source-butler-1863-press**. Butler’s 1863 newspaper text opposes accepting machine supremacy by calling for immediate destruction of machinery. It is an earlier held contesting response to the prospect, even though it also describes domesticated human survival rather than extinction.

### 114. Human minds transferred into machines — earliest-located

### 115. Human minds transferred into machines — earliest-located

Claim: `ht-w03-mcginn-1999-hello-hal`

McGinn's review of 1999 of books by Kurzweil, Moravec and Gershenfeld states that the authors take the mind to be essentially a computer, and that uploading into a machine might extinguish consciousness rather than transfer it.

Criterion: A human mind could be transferred to, copied into or continued in a machine substrate, so that human beings could themselves become machine intelligences.

Date: `1999-01-03`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. The New York Times Book Review, January 3, 1999.

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ht-w03-mcginn-1999-hello-hal-1`, Paragraph beginning "Kurzweil, Moravec and Gershenfeld" (quote); `evidence-ht-w03-mcginn-1999-hello-hal-2`, Paragraph on Kurzweil's uploading suggestion (quote).

Searches: `upload|conscious|identity|copy`.

Verdict: **stands**. Earlier held transfer scenarios describe copying or replacement; a discussion of identity puzzles within an otherwise favorable scenario is not an explicit denial that a machine continuation preserves mind or consciousness. McGinn’s January 3 review directly warns that uploading could extinguish consciousness. No earlier held qualifying counterargument was established.

### 116. Conflict over building superhuman machines — earliest-located

Claim: `ht-w04-degaris-1989-what-if-ai-succeeds`

De Garis's article of 1989 states that the artilect issue will divide humanity into two camps, which he labels "the Terras" and "the Cosmists", and that the conflict between them will be the dominant source of global political conflict in the 21st century. It states that the Cosmists will pursue a policy of mutual deterrence, so that the century will politically resemble the twentieth, with weapon systems "all the more horrific and artilectual".

Criterion: Disagreement over whether superhuman machine intelligence should be built will divide human beings into opposed political groups, and could lead to large-scale war between them.

Date: `1989`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Date of publication, as the source gives it.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ht-w04-degaris-1989-what-if-ai-succeeds-1`, p. 21, 'Scenario 1' (quote); `evidence-ht-w04-degaris-1989-what-if-ai-succeeds-2`, p. 21, 'The Cosmists' (quote); `evidence-ht-w04-degaris-1989-what-if-ai-succeeds-3`, p. 21 (quote).

Searches: `war|opposed groups|build|machine intelligence`.

Verdict: **stands**. Butler’s war is directed against machinery; it is not a political war between human groups divided over building superhuman intelligence. Earlier held machine-control and succession texts do not specify that division. The fixed 1989 magazine article explicitly names opposing groups and large-scale human political conflict.

### 117. Conflict over building superhuman machines — earliest-located

Claim: `ht-w04-yudkowsky-2000-re-hugo-de-garis`

Yudkowsky's Extropians list message of 22 June 2000, replying to a notice of a documentary on de Garis's "Gigadeath Artilect War", states that the projection of "Cosmists versus Terrans" is utterly unrealistic.

Criterion: Disagreement over whether superhuman machine intelligence should be built will divide human beings into opposed political groups, and could lead to large-scale war between them.

Date: `2000-06-22`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Date of posting.

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ht-w04-yudkowsky-2000-re-hugo-de-garis-1`, message body (paraphrase); `evidence-ht-w04-yudkowsky-2000-re-hugo-de-garis-2`, message body (quote).

Searches: `Cosmist|Terran|Artilect|war`.

Verdict: **stands**. Earlier warnings about machine misuse or succession do not explicitly contest the projected war between groups over building superhuman machines. The June 22 own reply specifically rejects that scenario. Do not substitute its quoted scenario for an earlier independent contrary argument.

### 118. Superiority by many orders of magnitude — earliest-located

Claim: `ht-w05-kelly-2017-myth-of-a-superhuman-ai`

Kelly's post of 2017 argues that intelligence is not a single dimension, so that "smarter than humans" is meaningless, and that there is no evidence that intelligence can expand without limit far above the human level.

Criterion: Machine intelligence could come to exceed human intelligence or overall cognitive capacity not moderately but by many orders of magnitude (a factor stated as trillions, or comparable to the gap between humans and much simpler organisms or matter). Capacity here means intelligence or overall cognitive capacity, not a robot's sensorimotor or output capacity.

Date: `2017-04-27`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. 27 April 2017, the date in the footer of the author's blog post.

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ht-w05-kelly-2017-myth-of-a-superhuman-ai-1`, List of five "heresies", item 1 (quote); `evidence-ht-w05-kelly-2017-myth-of-a-superhuman-ai-2`, Section 4 (quote); `evidence-ht-w05-kelly-2017-myth-of-a-superhuman-ai-3`, Section 4 (quote).

Searches: `orders of magnitude|vast|intelligence|limit`.

Verdict: **stands**. Earlier objections to particular AI techniques, software progress or technological autonomy do not establish an explicit upper bound on overall machine cognitive superiority. Lanier’s 2000 software argument is relevant context but does not directly answer the many-orders-of-magnitude premise as Kelly’s April 2017 discussion does.

### 120. Capital or the techno-economic process as machine intelligence — earliest-located

Claim: `ht-w06-negarestani-mackay-2018-reengineering-philosophy`

Negarestani, in his published conversation with Mackay of 2018, states that disconnecting intelligence from rational agenthood risks “rationalizing nature as intelligence” and that Land “mistakes rational agenthood with a perspectival phenomenal self”.

Criterion: Capitalism, the market or the techno-economic process is itself an artificial or machine intelligence, or the process through which machine intelligence assembles itself.

Date: `2018`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Urbanomic document UFD034, dated 2018.

Claimed: `2018-11`. The standfirst describes the text as an edited and expanded version of a conversation at a book launch in New York in November 2018; the conversation does not date the published wording.

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ht-w06-negarestani-mackay-2018-reengineering-philosophy-1`, Negarestani's answer to the question 'what does it mean to be on the side of intelligence?' (quote); `evidence-ht-w06-negarestani-mackay-2018-reengineering-philosophy-2`, Same answer, next paragraph (quote).

Searches: `capital|intelligence|rational agent|Land`.

Verdict: **stands**. Earlier critiques of acceleration, market outcomes or technological determinism need not deny the identification of the capitalist process with intelligence. The 2013 manifesto’s objection to capitalist speed concerns a different premise. The 2018 interview explicitly distinguishes intelligence and rational agenthood from such process-identification.

It is an earlier contesting prescription under this criterion, which includes restraining the process that produces machine intelligence. [Source](https://web.archive.org/web/20260826113039/https://paperspast.natlib.govt.nz/newspapers/CHP18630613.2.4).

Verdict: **move to source-butler-1863-press**. Butler’s held newspaper conclusion calls for stopping the machinery whose development is producing human successors. It is an earlier contesting prescription under this criterion, which includes restraining the process that produces machine intelligence.

### 125. Machine intelligence as a physical or cosmic process — earliest-located

Criterion: The rise of machine intelligence is an expression of a physical, evolutionary or cosmological process larger than humanity (such as thermodynamics, the evolution of complexity or a cosmic destiny of intelligence).

Verdict: **move to source-butler-1863-press**. ### 128. Machine substitution for labour produces explosive growth — earliest-located

Hanson, “Economic Growth Given Machine Intelligence” (2000-08-19) articulates G01. Criterion: The text states that if machines (AI systems or emulated minds) can substitute for human labour across all or nearly all tasks, so that labour can be produced like capital, the growth rate of output rises far above historical rates (by roughly an order of magnitude or more) or keeps rising. The conditions under which the text derives this are part of the record.

Verdict: **stands**. The 1998 fast-growth article requires broad improvement in capital but does not explicitly derive the required explosive output effect from making human labor reproducible by machines. ### 132. Essential unautomated tasks bottleneck growth — earliest-located

Claim: `ec-g04-nordhaus-2015-economic-singularity-a`

Nordhaus, “Are We Approaching an Economic Singularity? Information Technology and the Future of Economic Growth” (2015-09) articulates G04. Rapid improvement in information technology raises aggregate growth only if information substitutes well for other inputs in production, or for other goods in consumption. The inelastic case of the same model: growth converges to that of the slowest input.

Criterion: The text states that growth is constrained because essential tasks or inputs remain unautomated or unimproved, or are automated only slowly, so that automating many but not all tasks, or automating them gradually, does not by itself produce explosive growth (Baumol's cost disease; an elasticity of substitution below one between tasks or between information and other inputs). Reasons why automation stays incomplete or slow (technical difficulty, physical-world tasks, training data, regulation of AI training or deployment, slow adoption) belong here.

Date: `2015-09`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Cover "September 2015"; title page "September 1, 2015". NBER Working Paper 21547 carries "September 2015".

### 137. AI’s near-term aggregate productivity effect is modest — earliest-located

Claim: `ec-g08-acemoglu-2024-simple-macroeconomics-of-ai-a`

Acemoglu, “The Simple Macroeconomics of AI” (2024-04-05) articulates G08. A task-based estimate using Hulten's theorem: the share of tasks exposed to AI times the average task-level cost saving. ### 140. AI as a general-purpose technology with lagging productivity — earliest-located

Claim: `ec-m03-brynjolfsson-rock-syverson-2017-productivity-paradox-a`

Brynjolfsson, Rock, Syverson, “Artificial Intelligence and the Modern Productivity Paradox: A Clash of Expectations and Statistics” (2017-11) articulates M03. Argues that implementation lags, rather than false hopes, mismeasurement or redistribution, are the likeliest main explanation of the gap between AI expectations and slowing measured productivity. Concludes that AI breakthroughs have not yet diffused and their complementary changes depress measured productivity at first; the same conclusion calls AI the core of "a new, economically important potential GPT".

Criterion: A text that calls AI a general-purpose technology and states that measured productivity gains lag adoption because of complementary investment (for example a J-curve or a productivity paradox).

Date: `2017-11`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Cover "November 2017". Chapter in the NBER volume (2019). Checked in a current copy; an archive capture was not available, so earlier bytes are not authenticated.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`. Tier: {"value":1,"basis":"NBER working paper (core venue)."}.

Evidence: `evidence-ec-m03-01`, pdf page 2; abstract (quote); `evidence-ec-m03-03`, pdf page 36; Conclusion (quote).

Searches: `general.purpose technology|AI|complementary|lag`.

Verdict: **stands**. The 1999 IT-complementarity paper links workplace investment to productivity, but does not explicitly call AI a general-purpose technology. Earlier generic general-purpose-technology literature or adoption lags alone miss that conjunction. The November 2017 paper states both AI’s GPT status and delayed measured gains from complements.

### 147. AI’s productivity effect over the next decade — earliest-located

Claim: `ec-m08-acemoglu-2024-simple-macroeconomics-of-ai-a`

Acemoglu, “The Simple Macroeconomics of AI” (2024-04-05) articulates M08. Acemoglu applies a Hulten's-theorem task framework to exposure and cost-saving estimates to bound AI's ten-year TFP effect, against much larger forecasts (Goldman Sachs, McKinsey). Allowing for hard-to-learn tasks where AI gains are smaller lowers the ten-year TFP bound. Acemoglu converts the TFP bounds to GDP effects under two investment-response assumptions. The author-hosted version of 5 April 2024 gives no more than 0.71% over ten years, and less than 0.55% allowing for hard tasks; the NBER version of May 2024 gives 0.66% and 0.53%.

Criterion: A text that gives a stated estimate of AI's effect on aggregate productivity or GDP over a horizon of about ten years from the text's date. The figure and horizon are recorded; the proposition is neutral as to size.

Verdict: **stands**. Earlier held tier-1 estimates either concern a roughly twenty-year labor requirement horizon, investment/complementarity lags, or long-run explosive growth. Verdict: **stands**. Earlier explicit developer-race and product-safety arguments in Vinge, Armstrong and Hanson’s FOOM posts are outside tier 1. Nordhaus’s 2015 section discusses diffusion and misuse, rather than analyzing developer safety incentives. No earlier eligible tier-1 own developer-incentive argument was established; retain the November 2023 model and its competition assumptions.

### 154. Economics’ engagement with superintelligence, AGI and the singularity — earliest-located

Claim: `ec-e05-nordhaus-2015-economic-singularity-a`

Nordhaus, “Are We Approaching an Economic Singularity? Information Technology and the Future of Economic Growth” (2015-09) articulates E05. Nordhaus introduces the computer-science literature on AI and the Singularity to economists. Nordhaus surveys economic antecedents (Keynes, Simon and others) before his model.

Criterion: A passage articulates E05 only if it explicitly says something about the state of economics on the topic: that it is new, neglected, speculative, now on the agenda of economics, or beyond economists' existing tools. Economists' engagement itself is recorded under E01–E04 and in the timeline (the dated record), not here. Machine intelligence that does human jobs is not counted as AGI for E05, as for E02. Maintainer decision of 2026-10-15.

Date: `2015-09`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Cover "September 2015". Checked in a current copy; an archive capture was not available, so earlier bytes are not authenticated.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`. Tier: {"value":1,"basis":"NBER working paper (core venue); Nordhaus an economist."}.

Evidence: `evidence-ec-e05-07`, NBER WP 21547 (September 2015), sec. II "Artificial Intelligence and the Singularity", opening para., p. 2 (quote); `evidence-ec-e05-08`, NBER WP 21547, sec. III "Historical perspectives on Singularity in economics", p. 7 (quote).

Searches: `economics|Singularity|little writing|neglected`.

Verdict: **stands**. Hanson’s 2000 neglected-research observation concerns machine labor doing human jobs, expressly excluded from E05’s superintelligence scope. Earlier Hanson FOOM comments are tier 2. Nordhaus explicitly describes the state of economics on superintelligence/Singularity; the held Cowles capture provides an August 2016 witness rather than authenticated September 2015 bytes.

Date: `2024-01-25`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Publication date given on the RAND landing page for RR-A2977-2.

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ab-arg-d1-mouton-contests-1`, Conclusions, PDF p. 16 (quote); `evidence-ab-arg-d1-mouton-contests-2`, Conclusions, PDF p. 16 (the finding stated) (quote); `evidence-ab-arg-d1-mouton-contests-3`, Discussion, PDF p. 16 (quote).

Searches: `no statistically significant|uplift|capability frontier`.

Verdict: **stands**. The earlier GPT-4 card reports limitations but also positive informational uplift; it does not state a no-uplift result relative to alternatives. Soice’s qualitative-method caveat is not a contrary capability finding. Retain RAND’s January 25, 2024 result with its attack-planning scope. Its PDF returned HTTP 403 in this pass, so this decision rests on the dataset’s inspected passages rather than a claimed fresh full-text reading.

### 172. Public release of model weights increases biological misuse risk — earliest-located

Claim: `ab-arg-d6-kapoor-contests`

"On the Societal Impact of Open Foundation Models" (arXiv v1, 27 February 2024) states that across several misuse vectors, biological weapons among them, current research is insufficient to characterise the marginal risk of open foundation models relative to pre-existing technologies.

Criterion: The text states that making model weights publicly available increases the risk of biological misuse, or that some models should for that reason not be released openly.

Date: `2024-02-27`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Date of the arXiv v1 stamp and the paper's own dateline.

Stance: `contests_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ab-arg-d6-kapoor-contests-1`, Abstract, PDF p. 1 (quote).

Searches: `open weights|bioweapons|marginal risk|insufficient`.

Verdict: **stands**. Earlier general open-source arguments do not specifically contest biological marginal risk from released weights. Sandbrink’s restrictions and Seger’s extreme-risk argument have the opposite stance. Kapoor’s February 27 dateline and v1 stamp directly contest the adequacy of current biological-risk evidence; preserve that date despite the March-series identifier.

### 173. Published evaluations do not establish real-world biological risk — earliest-located

Claim: `ab-arg-d3-peppin-articulates`

The preprint "The Reality of AI and Biorisk" (arXiv v1, 2 December 2024) states that existing studies of AI-related biorisk are nascent, often speculative, or limited in methodological maturity and transparency.

Criterion: The text states that published evaluations or benchmarks of AI systems' biological capability do not, as conducted, establish the level of real-world biological misuse risk.

Date: `2024-12-02`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Date of the arXiv v1 stamp; the paper's front matter gives 4 December 2024 as its release date.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ab-arg-d3-peppin-articulates-1`, Abstract, PDF p. 1 (quote).

Searches: `criterion concepts across held titles, passages and accessible source bodies`.

Candidate: `source-eval-ontology-i-gpt4-card`. GPT-4 System Card

Document dated 2023-03-23; matching section 2.6 read in [2023-04-01 archive capture](https://web.archive.org/web/20230401010810id_/https://cdn.openai.com/papers/gpt-4-system-card.pdf). Current PDF has changed pagination and no cover date; do not substitute it for the early witness.

Locator: Section 2.6, capability-versus-likelihood caveat, PDF p. 12 in the archive edition. Passage (paraphrase): “The red-team work assessed capabilities, not the probability of weapon-development use, and GPT-4 access alone does not establish successful proliferation.”. The GPT-4 system card already separates assessed capabilities from the probability of a user employing them, and identifies physical-access requirements that information assistance alone does not satisfy. This explicitly limits inference from its evaluation to real-world proliferation risk before the December 2024 methodological review. It is a bounded caveat about this evaluation, not a verdict on every later benchmark. [Source](https://cdn.openai.com/papers/gpt-4-system-card.pdf).

Verdict: **move to source-eval-ontology-i-gpt4-card**. The GPT-4 system card already separates assessed capabilities from the probability of a user employing them, and identifies physical-access requirements that information assistance alone does not satisfy. This explicitly limits inference from its evaluation to real-world proliferation risk before the December 2024 methodological review. It is a bounded caveat about this evaluation, not a verdict on every later benchmark.

### 174. Assessment must cover actors who already hold relevant skills — earliest-located

Claim: `ab-arg-d7-brent-articulates`

The RAND working paper of 12 June 2025 states that safety assessments rely on benchmarks that overlook the full range of potential threat actors and fail to account for how AI could enhance the capabilities of skilled individuals as well as enabling non-experts.

Criterion: The text states that assessment of AI-related biological risk must cover actors who already hold relevant scientific skills, and not only actors without them.

Date: `2025-06-12`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Date of the arXiv v1 submission of the working paper, attested by the arXiv listing page; the paper's own cover gives only June 2025.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-ab-arg-d7-brent-articulates-1`, Summary, p. iv (PDF p. 4) (quote); `evidence-ab-arg-d7-brent-articulates-2`, Submission history block, v1 entry (quote).

Searches: `sophisticated actors|risk profiles|evaluations|monitoring`.

Candidate: `source-ab-sandbrink-2023`. Artificial intelligence and biological misuse: Differentiating risks of language models and biological design tools

2023-06-24 arXiv v1 versus 2025-06-12 v1 submission; keep the latter paper’s month-only cover distinct

Locator: Abstract, Table 1 and discussion of actor-specific design-tool risk mitigation. Passage (paraphrase): “Biological design tools increase sophisticated actors’ capabilities; these risks require monitoring and interventions tailored to such actors, alongside the novice-facing language-model risks.”. Explicit inclusion of skilled actors in risk monitoring and mitigation, not only novice uplift. [Source](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2306.13952v1).

Verdict: **move to source-ab-sandbrink-2023**. Sandbrink’s June 2023 v1 already differentiates nonexpert assistance from capability gains for sophisticated actors, requires monitoring design-tool risk and discusses actor-specific interventions. That puts skilled actors within the required risk-assessment scope before Brent–McKelvey’s June 2025 critique of incomplete benchmarks. It is not evidence that every earlier evaluation actually covered them.

### 175. Predicted protein structures aid drug design — earliest-located

Claim: `aib-arg-p1-blundell-1987`

The Nature review "Knowledge-based prediction of protein structures and the design of novel molecules" (Blundell, Sibanda, Sternberg and Thornton, 26 March 1987) states that knowledge-based prediction techniques are of value in predicting receptor structures to aid the design of drugs.

Criterion: The text states that protein structures obtained by computational prediction, rather than by experiment, are or will be of use in designing or discovering drugs.

Date: `1987-03-26`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Publication and issue date printed on the Nature article page.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-aib-arg-p1-blundell-1987-1`, Abstract (article page) (quote); `evidence-aib-arg-p1-blundell-1987-2`, Abstract (article page), what the techniques are (quote).

Searches: `predicted protein|receptor structure|drug design`.

Verdict: **stands**. No earlier held text located explicitly connects computationally predicted protein structures to drug design. Earlier inference, neural-network and chemical-structure work does not supply that protein-prediction-to-drug-design conjunction. The March 26, 1987 journal abstract states it directly; the claim concerns the text’s prospective usefulness argument, not demonstrated drug performance.

### 176. AI-discovered drug candidates succeed in clinical trials more often than historical averages — earliest-located

Claim: `aib-arg-p6-jayatunga-2024`

Jayatunga et al. (Drug Discovery Today, published online 30 April 2024) state that, in their analysis, AI-discovered molecules have an 80–90% success rate in phase I, substantially higher than historic industry averages, and a phase II success rate of about 40%, comparable to those averages, on a limited sample.

Criterion: The text states that drug candidates discovered or designed with AI have passed at least one phase of clinical trials at a higher rate than historical industry averages.

Date: `2024-04-30`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Electronic publication date as given by Europe PMC.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-aib-arg-p6-jayatunga-2024-1`, Abstract (Europe PMC record, abstractText) (quote); `evidence-aib-arg-p6-jayatunga-2024-2`, Abstract (Europe PMC record, abstractText) (paraphrase).

Searches: `success rate|historical|Phase I|AI.discovered`.

Verdict: **stands**. Earlier trial-entry announcements and favorable individual trial results do not compare phase-passage rates of AI-discovered candidates with historical industry averages. The April 30, 2024 electronic-publication abstract makes that comparison, with the small sample and phase-specific distinction retained. No earlier held comparative rate was located.

### 177. AI models can simulate or predict the behaviour of cells (a "virtual cell") — earliest-located

Claim: `aib-arg-p2-bray-1990`

Bray (Journal of Theoretical Biology, 22 March 1990) describes a procedure by which a parallel distributed processing (PDP) network could simulate a cell signalling pathway, and states that the analogy with PDP networks shows how living cells can recognise combinations of environmental influences.

Criterion: The text states that a machine-learning or other AI model simulates, or will be able to simulate or predict, the behaviour of a living cell, such as its growth or its responses to perturbations.

Date: `1990-03-22`; kind: `event`; basis: `publication`. Date of the journal issue in the PubMed citation (J Theor Biol 1990 Mar 22;143(2):215–231).

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-aib-arg-p2-bray-1990-1`, Abstract, third sentence (PubMed record) (quote); `evidence-aib-arg-p2-bray-1990-2`, Abstract, last sentence (PubMed record) (quote).

Searches: `cell|PDP|signalling|perturbation|simulate`.

Verdict: **stands**. Earlier artificial-neuron and connectionist texts model computation rather than explicitly simulating a living cell’s response. Older virtual-cell/chemical-kinetics wording without AI also misses the criterion. Bray’s March 22, 1990 abstract explicitly applies a PDP network to cell signaling; the criterion includes a response pathway and does not require a whole-cell model.

### 178. A drug candidate discovered or designed with AI has entered human clinical trials — earliest-located

Claim: `aib-arg-p3-berg-2013`

Berg’s news release of 21 November 2013 announces a phase Ib clinical trial at three named centres to study its investigational compound BPM 31510, which it describes as discovered and developed by its Interrogative Biology platform, “an artificial intelligence-based system”.

Criterion: The text states that a named drug candidate, described as discovered, designed or created using AI, has begun testing in humans in a clinical trial.

Date: `2013-11-21`; kind: `event`; basis: `announcement`. Date of the release (“Nov 21, 2013, 09:29 ET”); the announcement date is not the trial’s start date.

Stance: `articulates_proposition`; register: `nonfiction`.

Evidence: `evidence-aib-arg-p3-berg-2013-1`, Subtitle and opening paragraph (quote); `evidence-aib-arg-p3-berg-2013-2`, Paragraph on BPM 31510 and the platform (quote).

Searches: `named candidate|AI.discovered|clinical trial|BPM 31510`.

Verdict: **stands**. Earlier trial registrations can date a trial’s start but do not necessarily describe the named candidate as AI-discovered or designed. No earlier held text located supplies both parts. The November 21, 2013 release explicitly links the named compound’s human trial to an AI-based discovery platform; keep announcement kind and date rather than backdating the AI attribution to a trial start.

### PA-02

W03: agree that a self-reported modification date and another text’s archive capture are inconsistent evidence bases. Blind role 114 independently proposes a move to Hanson’s held Extropy print: year 1994, with a third-quarter footer, preceding both Minsky’s attributed October 1994 article and revised page dated January 2, 1997. This is stronger than ranking a February 1998 Hanson web capture against a 1999 Minsky capture, and does not claim the uninspected Minsky magazine wording is identical to the revised page.

Y22: agree that the November 13, 2000 message rejects automatic goodness rather than directly opposing speed. Differ on the implication that this necessarily disqualifies it: the collection explicitly allows a text that contests a proposition’s premise, and Y22’s written criterion includes good-by-default. Blind role 99 proposes an earlier premise-contesting text, the author’s August 1, 1999 reply withdrawing assured benevolence and describing uncertain survival odds. Neither passage should be rendered as a direct call to slow development. The distinction between a contrary premise and an explicit speed prescription must remain visible.
